# Round journal

What a round is and how it links to the rest — [../LOOP.md](../LOOP.md). The
record format — `../../skills/evidence-loop/specs/round.md`.

**The next round's number is the highest here plus one.** That is the only
source: not memory, not a commit message, not the user's words.

## The 251-265 gap, explained (curate after round 327)

`loop.py lint` has warned about a hole between 250 and 266 on every run for
around seventy rounds, and nobody had written down why. It is not lost data.

Those fifteen rounds were **one investigation** — B-22, the paused consumer that
never repaid the connection pool — and their findings were written into the LEAD
rather than into round files. The commits are all in git
(`docs(loop): round 263 …`, `264`, `265`), and
`../backlog/archive/B-22-paused-consumer-never-repays-the-pool.md` names 251, 252, 253,
254, 256, 262, 263, 264 and 265 by number: five witnesses that failed to observe
the defect and one reading that explained why.

So the gap is a record-keeping choice, not a gap in knowledge. Left as-is — the
content is reachable and renumbering would break every inbound reference — and
`lint`'s warning stands as a pointer to this note rather than to a mystery.

- **[608](608-a-third-of-a-percent.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart — **B-205: per-call log scopes are not worth optimising.** The five derivations a unary call makes cost `228 ns` against `66.8 us` for the call (0.34 %), and every name is a fixed literal or a registered method key, so the 512-entry level cache cannot churn short of 512 methods. `C-64`, `P-215`
- **[607](607-the-same-overhead-on-both-runtimes.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart_compression — **B-214: compare-and-keep-smaller holds with `RpcGzipCodec` on the VM and on node.** `32/64/128/192` incompressible bytes `42/74/138/202` off and on, both runtimes; both guards still shrink. The control, core forced to always compress, reads `62/94/155/206` on BOTH — the codec's small-message overhead is identical across runtimes, so the disagreement the lead was looking for does not exist. The test now lives where the codec does and runs on both targets
- **[606](606-eight-timers-for-disposers-that-cannot-hang.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-210: eight of a server stream's nine timers bounded disposers that cannot hang.** P-156 with each timer attributed to its stack: `RpcCallScope._close` wrapped EVERY disposer in `.timeout(5 s)`, synchronous ones included, across three scopes per call. Now only a Future-returning disposer is bounded: `9.1 -> 1.1` timers/call with no deadline, `14.1 -> 5.0` with one. **The first version also dropped the `await` for synchronous disposers and twelve cancellation tests went red** — a later disposer tears down what an earlier one's cancel notice still needs a turn for — so the await stays. Canaries: the old wrap reads `9.05`; no bound at all leaves a hanging async disposer blocking `close()`. `P-156`
- **[605](605-one-failure-one-record.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-124 closed: one caller record per genuine fault, on every shape.** The owner chose the call's own catch (error, method path, stack). Measured first, the shapes disagreed: `unary 2, server 2, client 3, bidi 0` — bidi's caller logged NOTHING on a real INTERNAL. Now `1` each: status sites and the inner `finishSending` catch go to debug, the bidi bridge gains the record, and `payloadResponses` (used without an endpoint, so the only record there) keeps error for faults only
- **[604](604-the-peer-chose-how-many-lines.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-124's flooding half, unwitnessed since round 515: the responder warned once per refused stream, so a peer chose how many lines it logged.** A raw client transport reaches five of the seven sites (half-open reclaim, stream ceiling, handler ceiling, pre-method, pre-bind); each warned `5` times for five refusals and now warns `1` per connection, the shape `_warnedRepeatOpeningFrame` already used. The two round 515 could not reach stay unguarded. Gate: one unidentified rpc_dart red at load 11-13 on the first run, green alone and on the second run
- **[603](603-every-streaming-answer-was-an-incident.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-124: a streaming NOT_FOUND was logged as an incident on both sides.** Round 516 fixed unary only; one NOT_FOUND produced `4`, `5` and `2` error records on server-stream, client-stream and bidi, from nine sites. All nine now consult `RpcStatus.isFault` (status sites) or the new `isFaultError` (catch sites), so an answer logs at debug and INTERNAL or a status-less throw stays at error. Two canaries, each bringing back exactly its own sites by message. Left in B-124: the per-call peer warnings, and the caller's double record for a real fault
- **[602](602-the-name-only-one-side-could-say.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-198: the caller refused service names the server routes.** `forClientRequest` capped each name at 128 while the responder routes paths to 1024, so `129`-`1018` characters was routable and uncallable; it now uses the responder's own grammar (`parseRpcMethodPath`), as `forClientRequestWithPath` already did, and the band builds. The same constructor admitted a DOT in the method name, which the server refuses — now refused by the caller. Canaries: the cap back refuses a 600-char service; the dot pattern and the path check off build `/Svc/Get.All` and an over-long path. One existing test now sees the refusal at the caller instead of the server; the server's refusal is still witnessed by its hand-built-frame test. `P-160`
- **[601](601-the-bidi-half-close-nobody-measured.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart — **B-218: the bidi mid-frame half-close, fixed by construction in 554, now measured.** `status 3` with the fix; with it ablated the bidi call ends as an EMPTY SUCCESS over a message the peer did send (server-stream: deadline; client-stream: `got:0`). Witness and guard added; no `lib/` change. B-218 narrowed to the client-stream caller and the memory number
- **[600](600-the-queue-was-the-bench.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart — **B-217 refuted: the connection queue's 355 MiB was the bench's own paused subscription.** Reading `BufferedBroadcastController` first: it buffers only with NO listener and has a 4096-event bound. Same 400 x 1 MiB direct objects: paused `+392`, consuming `+6`, none `+376` MiB. Every endpoint consumes; the no-listener case joins B-225 item 2. `C-63`, `P-214`
- **[599](599-the-token-captured-once.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **B-139 closed: `connect()` gets `headersProvider`**, called for the first upgrade and for every reconnect, so an expiring token can be refreshed (owner's request). Read on the server: `Bearer t1` then `Bearer t2`; the wrong neighbour, a provider evaluated once and cached, reads `t1` twice. Passing it with `headers` is an `ArgumentError`, witnessed. `connectTimeout` does not cover the provider's own time
- **[598](598-the-connection-nothing-refused.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **B-139 item 6d: `RpcWebSocketServer` gets `maxConnections`**, the first connection cap on any server in the repo (owner's request). Counted on live endpoints, so a closed connection frees its slot; over it the socket closes with 1000 and a reason, and the caller sees a retryable UNAVAILABLE. Three canaries: the check off serves the third client, counting connections EVER opened (the wrong neighbour) never frees a slot, and the warn-once guard off logs 5 warnings for 5 refusals
- **[597](597-the-callback-that-never-ran.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **B-139 split and decided; the server's construction gaps closed.** Passing both `onEndpointCreated` and `onPeerEndpointCreated` silently ran only the peer one, so the contracts registered in the other never existed; it is now an `ArgumentError` (owner's choice, breaking for code that passed both). `createWithContracts` could not take a `LogController` and now forwards one; the peer and responder branches of `_handleConnection` are merged. Each half witnessed by its own failing test. Item 7 read: the responder wrapper hides only `IRpcReconnectableTransport`, which nothing server-side checks. Left in B-139, both owner-requested: a connection cap and a per-reconnect headers callback
- **[596](596-the-rebroadcast-that-carries-nothing.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart_websocket — **B-203 refuted.** The wrapper "re-broadcasts every frame the core already routed" — measured over a real socket it forwards `0.00` events per unary call and per 100-message stream, the same as a bare channel transport, because it forwards only what the core broadcasts and round 508 stopped that for routed responses. The control is round 508's skip forced off in place: `3.00` and `101.00` on both, so the bench sees every frame when there is one. No code change, no gate run. `C-62`, `P-213`
- **[595](595-the-product-the-per-stream-bound-left.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-138 closed: the per-stream ceilings multiplied by the stream count.** Parked handlers on one connection held `8184` messages at 8 streams and `16368` at 16 against a peer ignoring flow control. The owner chose the cap: the connection window, which an honest peer cannot exceed. Now `4093` (64 MiB) at either count, client-stream and bidi, honest peer unchanged and never refused. Two release paths turned out load-bearing and are witnessed: a refused message must go back to the flow-control pool (the existing debt test wedged on the first run without it), and a stream torn down with frames held must return them (else the next call is refused). A third release was written, could not be made to fail, and was removed. Remainder in B-225. `P-211`
- **[594](594-the-two-buffers-the-stream-bound-never-saw.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-138 continued: two responder buffers the per-stream bound never reached.** With a peer ignoring flow control, a parked client-stream handler held `19999` of 20000 messages (~312 MiB) against `257` with an honest peer and `1023` on the bidi sibling; the sink is fed by the pipeline, not through the transport's bounded controller. The sweep of five request-buffer paths found the second: a unary state is never bound, so frames sent while its handler runs pile into the pre-bind list, RSS tracking the bytes 1:1 at three scales. Both now apply `effectiveMaxBufferedBytes` and `maxBufferedMessagesPerStream` and answer RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED: `1023` retained, unary refused. Writing the release witness found a regression the bound would have caused — a waiting unary also charged every fragment it was fed — fixed by routing fragments first. Six canaries, each witnessing its own half. Left: the connection total (4096 streams × 16 MiB). `P-211`, `P-212`
- **[593](593-the-pull-is-not-what-flow-control-bounds.md)** INCONCLUSIVE, rpc_dart — **B-138's remaining subject narrowed, three dead ends closed, not settled.** `PULLED 20001 of 20001` with flow control ON and OFF alike, 320 MiB offered on one peer-minted stream with the consumer paused. **The control not differing is the finding**: rpc_dart's flow control is a SEND-side credit protocol — it bounds what this side sends and asks the peer to stop by withholding grants, so against a peer that ignores grants it does nothing and it never throttles READING, which corrects the lead's own framing. **Not settled**: `+34 MiB` against 320 MiB offered with nothing refused means the frames are DISCARDED, and the likeliest cause is the rig — a peer-minted stream with no responder endpoint has nowhere to be dispatched, so `_admitToStreamBuffer` is not shown to have been reached. **Three rig failures recorded because each read as a result**: a `StreamController` source measured the BENCH (`+316 MiB` ON, `+300` OFF — the controller queueing 320 MiB with no peer waiting for credit); a data frame for an unopened stream is silently dropped, so feeding only data reads `delivered 0, errors 0` everywhere and looks like a bound; `health().details` carries no buffer depth. The instrument that works is the one `P-167` already established for this lead's first half, and the round had to rediscover it. `P-210` marked **broken** with the fix named. No code change, no gate run
- **[592](592-the-diagnostic-that-went-quiet-when-it-mattered.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **B-219, and the lead's instruction not to loop the gate is what made it cheap.** `health().details['peerStreamIds']` answered null under load where the same call had returned 0. The assembly sets the key unconditionally after the spread, so it cannot be missing there — which means the call returned from somewhere else, and it did: two early returns above it, `_closed` → `closed(...)` with no details at all and `_disconnected` → `degraded(details: {'supported': true})` with no counters. Both exist for documented reasons and **neither reason says anything about the wrapper's own counters** — the only thing in `details` no inner health can see — so they were dropped by omission on exactly the two answers a supervisor polls for. Reachable in that test without touching it: the heartbeat closes the socket when a pong misses its one-interval deadline. One `_ownDetails` getter on all three answers, degraded keeping its own key so the change adds rather than swaps. Canary reads `Actual: <null>` and NAMES the state, which the original `Null check operator` crash could not — and the test's `!` is gone, which was the lead's other half. `bench: none` by the lead's own direction
- **[591](591-the-refusal-pays-for-its-own-cleanup.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart — **B-207 REFUTED, and the lead itself said the round would be "mostly the measurement".** The mechanism holds — `_LimitedByteSink.add` throws and `sink.close()` on the next line is jumped over, 20000 of 20000 times — but nothing accumulates: `CONTROL +12/+30/+31 MiB` against `WITNESS +12/+6/-53` across three runs, so **the arm that reaches `close()` is the arm that grows**, and once the witness handed 53 MiB back to the collector mid-arm. **Structural rather than lucky: tripping the limit requires decompressing UP TO the limit**, so the refusal path generates the GC pressure that runs the finaliser in proportion to the limit it is about to breach — and "at a rate the peer chooses" needs a cheap refusal, which this path does not offer. The sketched `try`/`finally` was PRICED and declined: free of the hazard it looked like it had (`close()` after the throw is clean, the FormatException survives), but nothing witnesses it. No code change, so no gate run — `lib/` and `test/` byte-identical. `P-209`
- **[590](590-one-factory-one-name.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart + rpc_dart_websocket — **the one item in the core+websocket scope no round could take: `B-174` claim 4, decided by the owner as deprecate `.pair`, keep `memoryPair`.** Hypothesis was "a one-line annotation"; it is not, because `--fatal-infos` makes every internal use of a deprecated member fatal — **225 call sites, 97 files, 9 packages plus docs**, reported to the owner before being carried out. The forwarder holds no behaviour and always forwarded, so the whole cost is the sweep. The rewrite is a literal replacement run from a script that reports per-file counts, so the diff checks against the counts. **The gate then found a sixth member of B-224's family, in websocket**: `a_timed_out_connect_releases_its_socket` counted every TCP descriptor the PROCESS holds via `lsof -p`, and suites share a process — fixed by filtering on the black-hole address (RFC 5737 TEST-NET-1, which nothing else here connects to), which also made its ablation readable in 4 s instead of a 120 s test timeout. Canary B was first written to ablate the whole `connectTimeout` branch and went red by that timeout rather than by the assertion — a red for the wrong reason; removing only the private client is the pre-fix shape and reads `20`. Touches 9 packages against the one-package guidance, which a rename sweep cannot avoid
- **[589](589-every-arm-ablated.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart + rpc_dart_http — **the owner's actual question: do these tests catch regressions. Six for six, each ablated against its own mechanism.** `window: null -> waiters 0`, `the ending made a no-op -> 37 more messages (6 -> 43)`, `exact-growth buffer -> ratio 4.15`, `append before the cap check -> peak 67108864 against 1048576`, `_runDrain polled -> 53/52, 52/52, 51/51, diff 0`, `the _isClosed branch off -> 8 errors for 8 calls`. The two B-224 left needed the INVARIANT rather than a proxy: RSS is not attributable in a shared process, so `oversized_chunk_not_copied` now reads `peakReassemblyBytes` — a PEAK, because a completed frame compacts the buffer and `_failChannel` drops it, and **reading the current size made the ablation PASS**, which is how that was caught. An idle-baseline subtraction was tried first and still read 34.7 MiB at 32. The drain takes the DIFFERENCE between two drains releasing inside one 50 ms tick, best of three: polling quantises both and cannot produce the gap on any attempt. **A sixth member was found by the gate — round 587's own test**, which slept 300 ms to park 8 requests. Sweep `16 / 24 / 32 / 48` green, gate effective ~16; no serial lane needed. `P-208` reused
- **[588](588-the-gate-oversubscribes-its-own-cores.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **the owner asked why the tests had started failing, and round 587's answer was a guess that does not survive its own control.** 587 wrote "load average 11.37" and called it a flake; with 14 busy isolates in another process at **84.76**, eight times that, the arm reads clean. The variable is `dart test`'s own suite concurrency: `numberOfProcessors 8`, `dart test` default ~4, `melos exec --concurrency 4` → **~16 suites on 8 cores**, 2x oversubscribed and exactly at the threshold, which is why it fails intermittently rather than always. `--concurrency=16` green, `24` RED twice with different members, `32` RED. **The question that cracked it was the MARGIN**: the park arrives at `produced 66` and needs 5-25 ms against a 250 ms settle — 10-50x, not the 250x it looks like, because the handler awaits a `Duration.zero` TIMER per message. **It is a family of five**, each measuring a shared resource as if the test owned it; three fixed — two settles became condition waits with a ceiling (the ceiling is not the wait), and the ratio was INTERLEAVED, since minima suppress jitter within a batch and do nothing about drift between two: `24` read `15698 / 31040, ratio 1.98` against quiet `1.97`, both halves inflated 1.56x and the threshold untouched. Two left (the PROCESS's RSS, a 35 ms ceiling) need a serial lane, which is the gate's shape and the owner's. `P-208`, `L-19`
- **[587](587-eight-calls-eight-errors.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http — **B-143's last claim, and the sibling was the next branch down in the same `try`.** An orderly `close()` logged one `error` per in-flight call: `8 calls -> 8 errors`, `1 -> 1`, `0 -> 0`, the count read before the close as well so the arm asserts its own setup. "Each" being the claim is what made a SCALING count the only admissible arm — one record would have read as a message. `http.RequestAbortedException` is already logged at `internal` fifteen lines up, with the reason written out (*"logging it at error would make every ordinary cancellation look like a failure"*), and `close()` sets `_isClosed` before closing the client, so the flag was a reliable signal the catch did not read. After: `errors 0, internal 16` — **the probe admits `internal` deliberately**, because with the default level a zero at `error` cannot be told from a log DELETED rather than moved. **And the gate went red twice before it went green, on a core test this round cannot reach** — `the_window_counts_wire_bytes_test` at load 11.37, green alone and green on the third run; filed as `B-224` rather than re-run away, because its assertion is a PARK and reads `0` whether the window works or not. `P-207`
- **[586](586-the-header-core-had-to-filter.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http — **a comment claiming a requirement the same file contradicts fifty lines up.** `te: trailers` was sent with "Required by gRPC-over-HTTP/1.1 to signal trailer support", where the class doc says *"All response headers (including grpc-status) are in HTTP headers"* — no trailers to signal support for, and no such specification. The lead asks for a browser console (`B-215` puts that out of reach); the question a VM can answer is where the header GOES: `headers delivered 10, carries te YES` -> `9, no`, read on the responder TRANSPORT's metadata, other nine names unchanged, counted by the aggregate `maxMetadataBytes` check on every call. Hop-by-hop too, so a proxy strips it. **REFRAMED where the lead was wrong**: core's `_createContextFromMessage` excludes `te` by name, so no handler ever saw it — and that filter must stay, because `rpc_dart_http2` sends the header and the gRPC spec requires it. That is what put the fix in the HTTP/1.1 caller rather than in the filter. The browser claim stays a spec citation and is moot once the header is gone, which is the only reason this closes without a browser. `P-206`
- **[585](585-the-buffer-the-sibling-had-already-replaced.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http — **the lead named its own sibling and the sibling carried the reasoning in a field comment.** The caller buffered the request body in a growable `List<int>` and copied it again with `Uint8List.fromList`; the responder in the same package had moved to `BytesBuilder(copy: false)` and said why. `the buffer's own +205 MiB` for a 32 MiB body, now `+0`. **Two bare arms bracket it** — a plain `List<int>` and a plain `BytesBuilder` — because one allocation number says nothing about what it ought to be; before, the library sits with the list, after, with the builder. And the bracket is not a ratio: `LIST` read `+165` then `+528` on identical input, so what it establishes is which arm the library matches. RSS read three times in the library arm so the buffer is not charged for the payload the caller already held. Over HTTP/1.1 the whole body is buffered by construction, so this was every call. **The witness is IDENTITY, not RSS** — `identical(sent, payload)` is the same fact stated deterministically, and a test measuring resident bytes would fail on a busy machine. BREAKING: the payload is handed over. Not read: time, and the multi-chunk path's one remaining copy. `P-205`
- **[584](584-the-documented-setup-was-the-unsafe-one.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http — **the class's own example was the construction with every limit off, and the fix was a sentence its sibling had already written.** `WITNESS the policy parameter OMITTED -> 200, RSS +524 MiB` against `CONTROL const RpcSecurityPolicy() -> 413, +37`, 256 MiB as one declared frame. The STATUS cannot grade it: the pipeline reads the non-null getter, so an over-size body is refused either way — what differs is WHERE, and by then the transport's `BytesBuilder` has all of it. **Both orders**, the trailing control reading `+0`, because RSS does not come back down between arms. Sharpest form is inside one file: the response side reads the non-null getter, so the response was bounded and the request body was not, with four checks behind one `if (policy != null)`. `RpcHttpServer` has had `securityPolicy = const RpcSecurityPolicy()` and the doc line for it the whole time. **An explicit `null` is a separate arm from an omitted parameter** — a probe driving only the first measures the OPT-OUT and reports it as the default — and it is also the control that says the opt-out still works (`200, +454` after). The `bodyReadTimeout` half is filed rather than taken: its documented objection is about a SHORT budget (`500 ms -> 408`, `30 s -> 200 OK`) and the real reason is that the knob bounds the whole read, `B-217`. `P-204`
- **[583](583-the-row-the-table-was-missing.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart + rpc_dart_http — **a `cost` lead whose comment half was weaker than filed and whose unfiled half was a behaviour defect.** The comment names `_httpStatusToGrpcCode`, which does not exist, and `400 -> INVALID_ARGUMENT`, which is wrong — and the retry inversion it was arguing is TRUE anyway, because `INTERNAL 13 attempts 1` and `INVALID_ARGUMENT 3 attempts 1` are both final against `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED 8 attempts 3`. **The finding came from taking the table's own doc as a list to complete**: it says *"Two rows are kept beyond grpc-go's, each because something really produces it"*, and three statuses this responder produces had no row — 405, 408, 415. Two of them are the caller's own bug and `unknown` is already final, which is correct; the third is a stalled upload, and `408 -> 2, 1 attempt` became `408 -> 14, 3 attempts`. UNAVAILABLE and not the lead's other candidate DEADLINE_EXCEEDED, which names the CALLER's own budget and is not retryable either. **Canary B ablates the code the corrected sentence describes**, a comment having nothing to switch off — and it reports the thing the round did not depend on: the final-statuses GUARD still passes under it, which is the measurement that the conclusion never rested on the premise. Remainder filed as `B-222`: 413 is retried and a too-large body is uploaded three times. `P-203`
- **[582](582-two-answers-to-one-question.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http — **the lead prescribed its own instrument, correctly, and one instrument graded it wrongly.** `HANDLER 2 [application/grpc+proto, application/grpc]` against `WIRE 1 [application/grpc]`: the duplicate is real in the code's output and `dart:io`'s adapter keeps the LAST value, so no peer ever received two lines — nor the `+proto` the lead objected to, which is the value the adapter discards. **The consequence a peer actually had is none of the four filed claims**: a `+json` caller was told the bare `application/grpc`. Second home is `RpcMetadata.forServerInitialResponse()`, sent on every call by core's unary responder; what made the collision visible is the merge branch that exists so a repeated CUSTOM key survives as two values (round 545) — an overwrite would have hidden it. Fixed by picking an OWNER rather than sharing a helper: the transport resolves `content-type` from the request and skips any copy in the metadata, because core has no request in hand. The subtype is **rebuilt, not echoed** — the 415 gate checks only the `application/grpc` prefix, so the remainder is peer input landing in a response header. Graded `changelog`: the one wire-visible change is `+json` for a `+json` call, and the override that disappears is unreachable through the supported API. Two canaries, one per half; one arm PASSES canary B because its expectation coincides with the old literal. `P-202`, `L-18`
- **[581](581-the-policy-that-ran-one-way.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart + rpc_dart_http + rpc_dart_http2 — **the HTTP/1.1 mirror of round 567, and the fix needed two tries.** `1007` response headers delivered on a `maxHeaders` of 128, against a CONTROL at 9 — structural, because this transport is its own `IRpcTransport` and `_validateInbound` belongs to a class it is not; the REQUEST direction was already checked. **The first fix reproduced the very defect it guarded against**: reducing an offending frame to its status is right for a TRAILER and wrong for an INITIAL frame, since this transport routes `grpc-status` into the trailers — so answering the initial frame with our own INVALID_ARGUMENT put a manufactured status AHEAD of the server's real 9, `headers 4, grpc-status 3`. The probe caught it only because it reads the FIRST status, not the last. **One home for the rule**: `RpcSecurityPolicy.statusOnly` now owns what a caller keeps from metadata that breaks the policy, carrying 567's measurement, and core plus the http2 caller delegate to it — 567 left two copies and this would have been a third. Two canaries, one per half (`1007`, and `status 3`). `P-201`
- **[580](580-eight-claims-and-what-each-was-worth.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_isolate — **a lead whose own witness line reads "None", worked as a sweep: one verdict per claim, eight claims.** Below the severity bar and taken anyway, because leaving it open left eight unanswered readings in a package three rounds had just been inside. `closure capture TRUE but latent / startup-microtasks NOT EXAMINED / broadcast hop NOT EXAMINED / isolateId HALF TRUE / workerUri TRUE / runRpcIsolateManagerWorker ALREADY DOCUMENTED / finish unreachable TRUE / close() unawaited NOT EXAMINED`. **Claim 4's one-word summary could not say what was wrong**: `isolateId` is not unused — it builds `debugName` — what was dead is the COPY crossing the isolate boundary, which no worker-side reader touched. The arg is gone, the wrapper's indices moved down with it, and because the layout is positional and read by index the suite passing is what says nothing depended on the slot. **Claim 7 is documented rather than deleted, deliberately**: the branch below it DROPS what falls through, so deleting an unreachable branch would turn a bare end-of-stream message from a frame into a silent loss — a reading is enough to call it unreachable, not enough to make deletion safe. Claim 6 is REFUTED as a defect, already carrying its own "no-op on the VM, exists so the signatures match". Three claims unexamined and the whole web half unread, both named rather than quietly dropped. `bench: none`
- **[579](579-the-budget-that-was-never-doubled.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_isolate — **"a 30 s default is really 60 s" is false: `1.04x / 1.00x / 1.00x` across a 4x range of budgets.** Both timeouts exist and are sequential, but **nothing a caller controls can make the FIRST one slow** — the worker's wrapper sends its `SendPort` as its very first act, before a line of user code — so phase 1 costs the spawn itself and the real bound is `spawn + startupTimeout`. - **[553](553-the-parser-knew-all-along.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-126 closed on the third attempt, and the blocker the second died on was a GETTER.** `RpcMessageParser` already knew: every refusal path calls `clear()` before throwing, so leftover bytes mean "incomplete" and nothing else — `holdsPartialFrame` is `available > 0`, exact by construction, eight cases pinning it including both refusal paths. `fragmented -> got:64`, `mid-frame half-close -> status 3`, `LATE half-close -> status 3`, `whole -> got:64`, `REFUSED at a 1 KiB responder policy -> status 8 'max: 1029'` (the arm that reverted attempt 2), `streaming -> got:64`. **The design turns on asking the parser rather than the pipeline's own bookkeeping**, which stays true while a later fragment is being PROCESSED — answering the peer on that reading closed the responder out from under its own handler, silently, since a closed responder writes nothing. `P-181`, a tracer, is why the round converged: three statuses had been read and none said where the fragment went, and printing every record showed it delivered AND parsed with no answer after it. **A canary that PASSED is what improved the design** — a half-close was answered at three sites and a race picked one, so the redundant site went and each ordering now has exactly one; that also forced a new arm, because a half-close arriving after the wait begins is a different branch from one carried on the fragment itself. Behaviour change beyond the lead: a truncated unary request answers INVALID_ARGUMENT where it answered INTERNAL, and one existing assertion moved with it. - **[552](552-the-window-was-off-not-loose.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-195's decision was to DOCUMENT what `flowControlWindowBytes` bounds; carrying it out found the field bounding nothing in a legal configuration.** With `initialSendWindowBytes: null` a server stream was unbounded for life — `510806 msgs, sendCredit: 0`, no credit entry at all — against `4372 msgs` for every non-null value of that unrelated field. **An inbound end-of-stream was treated as the end of the CALL**, dropping the stream's flow-control state; on a PEER-opened stream it is their half-close, and a server stream half-closes its request immediately. `_advertised` is the only record such a stream exists, so `_onGrant` then discarded every grant as one for an ended call, and `tryConsume`'s re-seed from `initialSendWindowBytes` hid the whole mechanism at the defaults. - **[550](550-a-queue-depth-for-an-object.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-106's ceiling, taken on round 549's measurement.** `queue cost: HOLDING -33 MiB / MINTING 270 MiB / MINTING at depth 64, 71 MiB` — the bound lands where configured and costs the holding shape nothing, which is the decision's premise holding up. `RpcStreamBufferLedger` gained an EVENT dimension beside its byte one, both charged per message with whichever is reached first binding; `maxBufferedMessagesPerStream` defaults to 1024 and its doc states what it counts and why nothing else, which is B-209's and B-128's lesson applied BEFORE the field shipped. **Three places needed the count and not just the bytes**, each a defect on its own: `release` returns the event charge even for a zero-byte message — or a zero-copy stream is admitted `limitEvents` times and refused for ever, round 536's inversion — `forget`/`clear` drop it, and `trackedStreams` counts either dimension since a zero-copy stream holds events and no bytes. **The sharpest finding came from moving the probe**: there are TWO queues on the receive path, and measuring through `incomingMessages` read `355 MiB` with a depth of 64 configured, which looked exactly like a ceiling with no effect — the connection-wide broadcast is also sized by `bufferedBytes` and leaves `_streamControllers` empty, so the per-stream ledger was never charged. Filed as **B-217**, since that bound is per-CONNECTION and inventing it here is the mistake B-209 records. - **[549](549-a-pointer-or-a-payload.md)** DEFERRED, rpc_dart — **B-106's decision was MEASURE FIRST, and the measurement is in: both claims about a direct object are true, of different shapes.** `queue cost: HOLDING -1 MiB / MINTING 313 MiB`, for 400 objects of 1 MiB queued with no consumer. Five parts were needed, not the decision's three, and the arms it demanded both read green: `each frame split in two -> unary got:64`, `peer stops mid-frame -> status 3 ... the last gRPC frame is incomplete`. **Then the gate went red on five tests, and one of them has no fix inside this design**: a 32 MB payload compressed against a 1 MB configured policy answered `status 3 'closed mid-message'` where it must answer RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED naming `max: 1048576`. Both witnesses stay in the tree SKIPPED with the condition in the skip. `P-155`

- **[544](544-three-wires-one-field.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart + rpc_dart_http — **an owner decision whose PREMISE was refuted by pricing it before implementing it.** B-209 decided `maxMetadataBytes` "names the WIRE" and that `validateMetadata` should count the same quantity; there is no single wire. `text 1426 / json 2479 / http 1938` at 128 small headers, converging to within 1% at few large ones (the control), so per-header framing is `8.2 B` as JSON against exactly `4.0 B` as HTTP header lines and any surcharge in the shared policy is right for at most one transport. **And the JSON size depends on a value's CONTENT**: the same 100 characters cost `127` or `227` bytes depending on escaping, and a quote is printable ASCII — so the decision was not implementable from lengths at all. Severity was also far lower than the lead implied: the inbound frame's declared length is already bounded against the field before buffering, and the HTTP responder has its own aggregate bound, so the quantity a peer controls is checked and the gap is `maxHeaders` x framing — 512 bytes against 64 KiB. Put back to the owner, who revised it to **keep the text count and document it**, as B-195 got. Carried out, plus the stale prose found on the way — `validateMetadata` still claimed "the HTTP transports validate here and reach no such check", which round 524 had made false in that very function — and two measurements moved out of `lib/` to the journal. **The first canary PASSED**, which is the useful part: the witness used one large header, where 8 bytes moves nothing, so an arm with 64 small ones had to be added before a per-header surcharge could be seen. Two arms now, one per way the decision could have gone, each caught by its own canary and by no other. `P-176`
- **[543](543-the-encoding-nobody-could-perform.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **the owner asked why the tests were failing; three causes, and the one a previous round had written off as a bad test was a library defect.** `caller_pipeline` declared `grpc-encoding: gzip` from a CONSTANT six lines above a block that built `grpc-accept-encoding` from the REGISTRY, so wherever no gzip codec is registered the caller announced an encoding it could not perform, the peer refused it UNIMPLEMENTED, and **every call failed** — making `compressionEnabled: true` unusable on dart2js, which is that state by construction. `requestEncoding()` now asks the registry, preferring gzip so the shipped default is byte-identical, and declaring NOTHING when nothing is registered. **The measurement's own trap**: P-175's first version used `memoryPair` and reported all four arms healthy, because the declaration is guarded by `!supportsZeroCopy` and a zero-copy pair never reaches it. **B-211 was wrong and the way it was wrong is the lesson**: it concluded "the LIBRARY is behaving correctly" from the error STRING, with nothing varied — rule one, an error string is prose about code. One `unregister('gzip')` on the VM settles it in a second. The other two failures: `rpc_dart_http`'s cancel test is a PREMISE broken by round 536's abort (`[]` at 0 and 1 ms, `[/Svc/slow x4, []]` at 5 ms, clean from 20 ms — it now polls for arrival), and a THIRD red that `set -e` had hidden behind core's suite since round 513, where one `dart test` invocation starts a second Chrome while the first shuts down. **`test:web` is still RED and an earlier draft of this record claimed it green** — the clean-looking run was piped through `grep`, so the status read was the pipe's; core's suite IS green, and the remaining red is `rpc_dart_isolate`'s Chrome suite, which fails differently every run (the same file passed alone in 6 s and later failed alone in 95 s while the load average climbed 7.5 -> 20.5). B-215. `P-175`
- **[542](542-the-ceiling-that-was-a-fallback.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **`global` was the last entry in a first-match chain, so a call with any more specific limit was not subject to it at all.** B-111's remaining half, the owner's decision. `global: 5` with a looser `perMethod` admitted `100 / 100`; it now admits `5`, and the new arm reads it from the other side — `global 5/s + perMethod 10/h` went from `10, then 0 after the window rolled` to `5, then 5`. `_resolveCounter` became `_resolveSpecific` and stops before the global counter; `_tryAcquireAll` requires BOTH to admit, so the tighter of the two binds in either direction. **The second half is the one the decision did not name**: two counters cannot be consulted at one instant, so a call global refuses has already charged the specific counter — and under sustained overload that drains a limit which was never reached, TIGHTENING it, the opposite of the defect. `refund()` inverts `tryAcquire` exactly for both algorithms (the sliding window's current bucket, the token bucket clamped to its burst). Its canary is the one worth having: without it the fix passes every arm that measures admission. Breadth is one site — `grep` for `perMethod` across every package's `lib/` returns this file alone. `_statusResourceExhausted = 8` went too, a second home for `RpcStatus.resourceExhausted`. **A gate failure this round could not name**: `test:unit` failed once in `rpc_dart_http` at a 15-minute load average of 10.33 and the reporter's lines were gone before the re-run, which passed twice; recorded as UNEXPLAINED rather than as a flake, because naming it first is the rule. Remainder: B-213. `P-140`
- **[541](541-the-number-that-belonged-to-somebody-else.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket + rpc_dart — **a caller was handed another call's ANSWER.** B-199 measured at the transport alone; driven through the real pipeline, the reused peer id is worse than filed: the caller asking `two` received `answered one`, from a different caller on a connection that no longer existed, and its own request was never dispatched. Controls: no reconnect (ids 2 and 4) and a reconnect with nothing left parked, both `answered two`. **Three mechanisms, each found by fixing the previous one and re-measuring, each with its own canary.** (1) The wrapper keeps `incomingMessages` stable across a reconnect by design, so the pipeline never learns the socket dropped — `_abortActiveStreams` never runs, the old stream state survives, and the new call's opening frame on the same number reads as a repeat and is IGNORED. Fixed by saying it in the protocol the responder already speaks: the peer's own `x-client-cancelled` notice, emitted before the reconnect's awaits so the reclamation has the whole handshake to run in. (2) `UnaryResponder.close()` cancelled subscriptions and nothing else, so a parked handler resumed and sent its late CANCELLED trailer on the old id — `status 1: WebSocket reconnecting` to a caller that had cancelled nothing. The other three shapes had this already via `StreamProcessor._isActive`. (3) `_ensureUnaryResponder` ends with `await _cleanupStream(streamId)` AFTER the handler, so it closed the NEW call's responder and released its id mid-answer; `_cleanupStream` now takes the state it was issued for, at **13 of 18** call sites, the other five being bulk teardowns where no new call can take the number. **Also found**: `melos run test:web` has been red since round 513 in one compression test (B-211), and a rig error worth keeping — a caller whose socket goes away under it reports its failure to the ROOT ZONE if the handler is attached later than the call. `P-174`
- **[540](540-the-value-that-never-left.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http — **one direction implemented the rule the other assumed.** The response side splits metadata on `,` and argues carefully that this is the recombination the field's own definition provides for; the request side then wrote `map[name] = value` and DROPPED a value instead of producing that encoding — two values of `x-tag` reached the server as `[second]`, and now read `[first,second]`. The response half is also confirmed (`date` arrives as `"Mon"` + `"29 Sep 2026 12:00:00 GMT"`, `www-authenticate` cut inside its quoted string, with single-value rows as the control) and **split to B-200**, because the sketch's rule needs a way to tell a custom key from a standard one and the wire carries no such marker: either a maintained standard-field list whose every gap is this defect again, or stop splitting — both change what callers observe. **Three rig errors recorded**: `RpcMetadata` rebuilt from `.headers` drops `methodPath`, so the probe's first run fired no request at all (round 488's trap); `['Mon', '29 Sep …']` and `['Mon, 29 Sep …']` have the same `toString()`, so the second run read the split as INTACT — a list print cannot answer a question about how many values there are; and round 539's process-wide fd count passed alone and failed in the full suite, because `dart test` runs suites as isolates in ONE process. `P-173`

- **[538](538-the-documented-composition-never-worked.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http — **a class that says three times it "mounts on any shelf server or router", one line above the read of the path a mount is defined to change.** Mounted at `/rpc`, the transport saw `/rpc/Echo/echo` and answered `status 3: Invalid method path`; the unmounted CONTROL answers `ok: echo:hi`. Two readings per run — the `methodPath` names the cause, the outcome says it matters — because a wrong path and a missing service produce the same status from different causes. **The mount is built from shelf's OWN primitive**, `Request.change(path:)`, which is what `shelf_router.mount` does: the composition under test is the real one and nothing was added to the dependency list to measure it. - **[537](537-the-drain-that-always-threw.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http — **an external lead reopened a negative; the negative held, and its other half was true but harmless.** B-141's first claim is `checked/C-31`'s question from round 273, re-run rather than cited: `408` to the peer and `384 KiB` of socket buffer, reproducing round 273's number exactly, against a CONTROL that takes `4096 KiB` and answers nothing — the read does stop, because the body is still attached when the response completes and dart:io detaches it then. - **[536](536-the-release-that-freed-only-bookkeeping.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http — **a teardown that freed the ACCOUNTING and not the work**, the third form of RPC-14 in seven rounds. `releaseStreamId` removed `_pending`, `_activeStreams` and the id while the POST and the body read ran to completion: `40 of 40` chunks written and the server FINISHED, whether the call was abandoned at 300 ms or left alone. **The two arms being identical is the finding** — and it is legible only because the control separates after the fix, `21 of 40 / not finished` against `40 of 40 / finished`. **Read at the server, because the client cannot tell**: a dropped future reports nothing locally either way. - **[535](535-the-grab-bag-graded-itself-backwards.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **the second grab-bag from this intake whose own severity grading is inverted.** B-139 says "items 1 and 6's unhandled future are behaviour, not style". **Item 1 costs nothing**: `0ms / 0ms / 0ms` for the shipped close order, the swapped order, and the raw dart:io close as CONTROL — cancelling the subscription does not stop the handshake, because the SDK's close and ping machinery sits BELOW the subscription, in the transformer rather than the listener. **Item 3 does not exist**: one `@override`, not two. - **[534](534-the-pause-that-bounded-only-delivery.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **a paused consumer IS a limit, and it bounded only delivery**: with the consumer paused, `5000` chunks were still pulled from the source and `0` delivered, against `5000/5000` unpaused and `5000/5000` resumed. `_incoming` had no `onPause`/`onResume`, so everything arrived and sat in a controller bounded by nothing. **The witness the lead asked for would not have worked** — "RSS" is the reading P-128 established is noise across arms here, and "a paused consumer" does not exist anywhere in rpc_dart — so it was reframed from memory to DEMAND: an `async*` source counting its own yields, which suspends while paused, makes the answer a count. Both columns are read because the defect is that they disagree. **The layer below already did it right**: dart:io wires its own controller's pause to the socket subscription, so the chain was complete except for this link, which is also the argument that forwarding is safe rather than novel. **Graded as a CONTRACT gap, not a repair**: nothing in rpc_dart pauses a channel, so no behaviour in the library changed — it closes the `IRpcChannel` promise that `incoming` is an ordinary Stream, and the lead's `cost` grading was right. **The lead's real subject is still open** — what bounds a flood from a peer outside rpc_dart's flow control — and `IRpcChannel`'s own doc example has the same gap. `P-167`
- **[533](533-the-timeout-that-stopped-waiting.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **RPC-14's canonical shape, in a file whose own comment already named it.** `openWebSocket` said out loud that `Future.timeout` abandons the AWAIT and not the WORK, and handled only the half where the work succeeds LATE; the other half leaves **one descriptor per abandoned connect**, held until the OS gives up — `40` TCP fds from 40 timed-out opens to an RFC 5737 black hole, against a CONTROL of `1` for 40 that succeeded and closed, from a baseline of `0`. **Counted with `lsof` from outside Dart**, because a held descriptor leaves the process working, the socket invisible to Dart and the attempt failing on time; both of the rig's assumptions are ASSERTED (all forty settled as `TimeoutException`; a missing `lsof` exits 2) since either failing silently yields zero. **Cancelling needs something that OWNS the work** — a `Future` owns nothing — so a bounded open now gets its own `HttpClient`, closed forced on failure and plain on success, where the upgraded socket has already been detached. **Three ablations, and the first two were wrong about which change matters**: plain close instead of forced still passes, `connectionTimeout` removed still passes, the SHARED client fails at 20 of 20 — each of the first two left the other mechanism standing. - **[532](532-a-probe-is-not-a-failure.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **the refusal was correct; the REPORT was the cost.** Ten plain GETs to the websocket port produced **10 error-level records and 10 `onConnectionError` calls**, against a CONTROL of 0 and 0 for ten real handshakes — so a load balancer doing its job fills an operator's error log at its own probe rate, indistinguishable from real connection failures. Mechanism read from the SDK: `_upgrade` answers 400 and returns `Future.error(WebSocketException(...))`, which `bind` forwards to its OUTPUT stream, which is the server's `connections` stream. **Every arm also reads the status the peer received**, because silencing a report and removing it look identical from the log side and the second is worse. **The sketch needed two corrections and an existing test caught each.** Shape-before-gate answers a cross-origin probe `400` — "wrong shape" about a request refused on identity — and `origin_guard_test` pins 403, so the gate runs first. And the drain cannot be skipped: `await response.close()` on a request with an unread body never completes, so the peer got nothing, caught by `a_refused_upgrade_has_a_deadline_test`. **The fix widens a bound and says so**: rejecting non-upgrade requests here means draining them here, so a hold that existed only on gated servers now exists on all of them — same bound, wider surface. `P-165`
- **[531](531-the-restart-that-ran-inside-the-shutdown.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **a flag flipped BACK inside the window it was guarding.** `_isRunning` means both "accept connections" and "no shutdown in progress", and `stop()` clears it before doing any of its work — so a `start()` in that gap set it again and returned while the stop carried on, handing the restarted server's new connections to the old shutdown. Both halves of the lead confirmed, one per phase: a connection accepted during the DRAIN is `torn down` while `isRunning` reads true; one accepted during the CLOSE loop is `never` closed and **held by nothing** — `_endpoints.clear()` dropped it, so no later `stop()` or `dispose()` can reach it, which is the forgotten-socket state `_handleConnection`'s own comment refuses peers to avoid. **Read as the KIND of ending — refused, torn down, never — because a bool collapses three states to two** and the first two are different defects. **A probe arm was silently wrong first and that is the method finding**: the drain arm had no in-flight call, so `_drain` returned on its first poll and the arm became a duplicate of the close arm — two identical rows reading as a consistent result rather than a broken rig, and the lead's first claim was confirmed only once a real slow call existed. Fixed by serialising `start()` behind the published stop; **"refuse start while stopping" proved unnecessary** because `_handleConnection` already answers a mid-shutdown peer. Controls `after` and `nothing` are what make `endpoints held: 0` mean a leak. `start()` can now block for the whole drain budget: CHANGELOG line. `P-164`
- **[530](530-one-dead-peer-delayed-every-other.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **a correct per-peer timeout became an incorrect total the moment it was summed.** `stop()` awaited each endpoint's close in a loop, and a socket close is a handshake with the peer — so shutdown was the SUM of N independent waits on N different parties: `316ms / 1512ms / 6057ms` for 1 / 5 / 20 peers at 300 ms each, against a CONTROL of `1ms` for twenty instant closes. That control is what says the cost is the close and not per-endpoint bookkeeping; without it a change that sped up the bookkeeping would have read as the fix. **The per-peer cost is a stand-in and the record says so** — the real one is dart:io's close timeout for a peer that never answers, seconds rather than milliseconds, which would put twenty dead peers around a hundred seconds but needs a raw TCP peer to reproduce. **`Future.wait` is not a drop-in for a loop of awaits**: it abandons the remaining futures on the first error, so the failure is caught PER endpoint, and the witness carries a second assertion the probe cannot — every sink recorded its close was CALLED, because abandoning them is fast too. - **[529](529-the-constant-that-described-another-function.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **a documented keepalive, absent on one of the two construction paths, because a constant was read outside the scope its own doc states.** A hand-built `IOWebSocketChannel` with `pingInterval: 200ms` noticed a dead peer NEVER: dart:io was never told the interval (only `openWebSocket` sets it, and only `connect()` calls that) and the app-level heartbeat was off because `platformHonoursPingInterval` is true on the VM — a constant documented as describing `openWebSocket`, consulted by a constructor that never reaches it. **The lead's fix could not be built**: applying the interval to the passed socket needs the socket, and `IOWebSocketChannel`/`AdapterWebSocketChannel` expose no accessor, so the constructor can neither set a native ping nor ask whether one runs. It now defaults to "nobody is pinging this socket" — all it can know — and `connect()` states the constant at the one call site where its premise holds. **A control was WRONG first and the reason is the keeper**: a dart:io `WebSocket` answers a ping frame ITSELF, so a server that answers no RPC is still a LIVE path for a native keepalive; the arm asserting native detection there would have failed the fix for an unrelated reason, and was rewritten to assert the invariant that bounds it — `connect()` gains no second probe. Behaviour change on a published package: wants a CHANGELOG line
- **[528](528-the-code-the-peer-never-sent.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **a close code that names the peer, for a failure the peer had nothing to do with.** A TCP reset reached the caller as `status 13` "closed by peer with code 1002" — INTERNAL, which `RpcRetryInterceptor` does not retry — against `14` for a vanished peer and an orderly goodbye, and `13` for a 1011 the peer really sent. **The mechanism is read from the SDK, not inferred**: `websocket_impl.dart`'s socket `onError` calls `_close(WebSocketStatus.protocolError)`, assigns that to `_closeCode`, and closes the controller WITHOUT adding an error — so a socket failure is indistinguishable from a protocol close. And this library cannot send 1002 (an application may only send 1000 or 3000-4999, so its own framing code is 4400), which makes the local path the only source between two rpc_dart peers. **The reset is produced, not simulated**: Dart has no SO_LINGER, so the rig relays bytes, stops draining the client and destroys the socket — closing with unread bytes in the receive queue is what sends RST — and the arm asserts the close code it got, so a rig that degraded to FIN cannot read as a pass. The lead's second half held too: a raw channel error reached the caller as `WebSocketChannelException`, no status attached, which is the WEB shape since `HtmlWebSocketChannel` raises where dart:io closes. Both fixed, each with its own canary; the controls pin the change to 1002 rather than the table. **This LOOSENS**: a peer genuinely sending 1002 is now retried, bounded by the retry policy — a behaviour change wanting a CHANGELOG line. `P-162`
- **[527](527-the-guard-the-peer-can-defeat.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **the reconnecting wrapper's stale-id guard is sound for the ids it mints and defeated by the ids it does not.** Two things measured at once, for the same stale id at the same moment: a GUARDED operation (`sendMetadata`) and an UNGUARDED one (`returnFlowCredit`), against the one variable the guard cannot see — whether the peer reopened the number. The flow-credit pair reached the new socket on **every** arm, including the two where the send was correctly dropped, so B-131 is confirmed and fixed with the same `_liveHere` guard. But **rows 1 and 2 differ only in the peer's reuse**, and row 1 delivers a stale teardown to the call that now holds the number: `_liveHere` is membership, the reconnect clears both sets, and the peer's first frame puts the id straight back. Ids this side mints are disjoint by `resumeStreamIdsAfter`; the peer numbers its own and restarts at the bottom, and a bare `int` carries nothing that could tell the two apart. **Two of the lead's three claims did not survive**: `getMessagesForStream` needs no guard (a subscription is taken at setup, the inner transport already answers a dead id with a controller nobody feeds, and failing closed on a DELIVERY path turns a wasted subscription into a handler that never receives a request), and the guard it asks for does not close the scenario it describes. Controls: no reconnect at all (so `dropped` means a guard, not a dead pipe) and this side's own id space (where the guard holds). First round to sweep **RPC-03's inbound direction**, which the lens named and nobody had looked at. Split to **B-199**, where round 218 already rules out the cheap fix. `P-161`
- **[526](526-the-break-that-was-the-contract.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart — **B-129's severity is wrong in BOTH directions, and this item is the other one.** Its `break` after the first decoded unary response is the gRPC contract — a unary response is exactly one message, a server sending two is misbehaving, taking the first is what a gRPC client does — and the "extra response" warning below it is **reachable**, by the ordering the lead did not consider: a DATA frame arriving AFTER the status, where `completer.isCompleted` is true on the chunk's first message and the `else` runs. **Reading both orderings is what settles it**; the "unreachable" claim comes from following one path and concluding about the branch. The real gap is narrower than filed — a second response inside ONE chunk, before the status, is dropped without a word, while two split across chunks with a status between them IS reported — and closing it is one line with no defect behind it, since dropping is correct and only the silence is arguable. **Nothing was RUN, and the record says so.** Taken with 524 and 525 this settles the argument round 521 opened: item 14 was a DoS surface filed as hygiene, item 15 a reachable interop failure filed as hygiene, item 16 a defect that is neither. **A grab-bag lead does not merely under-state severity; it randomises it** — which is a stronger case for splitting the remaining fifteen than 521 could make. `checked/C-60`
- **[525](525-routable-and-uncallable.md)** DEFERRED, rpc_dart — **the second split out of B-129, and the band is now measured: `129` to roughly `1018` characters of service name is routable by the policy and REFUSED by `forClientRequest`.** A responder can register and route a name this library's own caller cannot construct a request for, and package-qualified protobuf names reach 128 characters without anything unusual — so the realistic case and the failing case are the same case. **Each row asks BOTH questions** — routable, and buildable — because a disagreement between two limits is invisible when either is read alone; the control is a name past the policy's own 1024, where both answers turn negative together, which is what makes the band a disagreement rather than the limits agreeing all along. **Not the one-liner the sketch implies**: `forClientRequest` is a STATIC with no policy in scope, which is presumably why the constant exists, so the fix is either threading the limit in (changing a widely used signature) or dropping the token cap and relying on `parseRpcMethodPath`, which already enforces the real limit — on that reading the 128 is redundant rather than wrong, but establishing it needs every caller checked for one that never reaches a parse. **And one question comes first: what was the 128 meant to be?** Moving a validation constant without knowing is how a limit ends up wrong in the other direction. Filed as **B-198**. `P-160`
- **[524](524-the-sum-nobody-was-taking.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **the fix round 523 declined to ship ungated.** `8 headers x 8192 B` now refuses at `65620 > 65536` where it previously passed at 1.0x of `maxMetadataBytes`, and `64 headers` at 8.0x likewise. A running total **inside** the existing loop rather than a sum taken afterwards, for round 506's reason: refusing after totalling the list would bound what is RETAINED rather than what is accepted, so a megabyte of legal headers is now refused at 64 KiB. **The count check still runs first and keeps its own message** — many tiny headers is a different fault from too many bytes. **Four guards stop this being a fix that merely refuses more**: metadata within the limit still passes, an oversized single header is still refused for its OWN reason, too many headers is still refused BY COUNT, and a policy with a larger `maxMetadataBytes` admits correspondingly more — so the bound follows the field, not a constant. Left undone and recorded: the two layers count different bytes (`validateMetadata` header text, `RpcChannelFrame` the encoded blob), the same confusion round 520 found in `maxActiveStreams`; it tightens, so it wants a CHANGELOG line; and the HTTP transports were never driven, so "a policy that does not bound" was never turned into a measured DoS. `P-159`
- **[523](523-the-knob-that-is-off-by-sixteen.md)** DEFERRED, rpc_dart — **the first split out of B-129, and it is a DoS surface that lead files under "read and delete".** `validateMetadata` bounds each header and never the total: `64 headers x 8192 B = 524818 B, 8.0x the metadata limit, ACCEPTED`. **The 128-header row is refused by the header COUNT, not by size** — which is the sharp part, because it means the effective ceiling is `maxHeaders x maxHeaderValueBytes`, about 1 MiB, and `maxMetadataBytes` bounds nothing that `maxHeaders` does not already bound worse. The knob is off by 16x in the direction that costs memory. **The control is load-bearing**: one oversized header IS refused, so four rows of ACCEPTED are about totals rather than about nothing being checked. **The channel transports are covered by accident** — `_decodeAt` bounds the encoded blob, which round 506 moved above the completeness check — **and the HTTP transports are not**, validating through the policy and never reaching that decoder. Filed as **B-197** with the number. `P-159`
- **[522](522-a-defect-masked-by-a-second-mechanism.md)** INCONCLUSIVE, rpc_dart_websocket — **the structure is confirmed in four lines and no witness can exist.** `_inner.createStream()`, an `await`, then `_inner.releaseStreamId(...)` — the field re-read after the suspension, the RPC-16 shape rounds 505 and 510 both witnessed and fixed. **This one is masked**, and the lead says why: *"harmless only because id resume keeps the ranges apart"* — the new transport resumes past the old one's last id, so the stale release names an id never minted and is a no-op. A witness would have to disable id resume first, and would then demonstrate a defect in a configuration the library does not ship: that is the claim *"safe only because of X"*, not *"broken"*. **The one-line fix was deliberately NOT applied** — `final inner = _inner;` is obviously correct and has nothing to canary, and one line with no evidence behind it is the one thing the loop does not do. What it would buy is independence rather than repair, which is the lead's own argument (*"any change to id resume makes the heartbeat release a live call's id"*) and therefore the owner's call.
- **[521](521-a-cleanup-list-with-defects-in-it.md)** INCONCLUSIVE, rpc_dart — **the lens turned on the LEAD, not the code.** B-129 frames eighteen items as "each is small; together they are the reading cost", witness "None — read and delete". That is true of the hygiene and false of at least four: **item 14** is a DoS surface (`validateMetadata` never bounds `maxMetadataBytes` in TOTAL — 128 headers of 8 KiB pass, and only the channel decoder bounds it, so the HTTP transports do not), **item 15** a reachable interop failure (tokens capped at 128 chars against a 1024 path limit), **item 16** silent message loss. **A round taking this lead at its word would delete dead code and leave a DoS surface in place.** Item 13 was measured: `cancel DURING backoff -> status 1 after 2118 ms` against `no cancel -> status 14 after 5237 ms`, so the call does NOT sit out `maxDelay` and the claim is refuted — **but two numbers are unexplained and the round says so rather than rounding them off**: a ~1.5-1.8 s lag between cancelling and completing, present even at t=0, and an uncancelled baseline of 5237 ms where five attempts at 4 s implies ~16 s. Hence INCONCLUSIVE rather than CLEAN. **The never-cancelled control was added second and is what made the table readable** — with only the two cancel arms both read ~1.7 s and there was nothing for them to be shorter THAN. `P-158`
- **[520](520-two-sides-counting-different-things.md)** DEFERRED, rpc_dart — **CONFIRMED, and the round's method is the finding as much as the result.** With the ceiling on the CLIENT only, four calls parked awaiting responses leave four slots free and a second batch of four is admitted in full — `0 refused`. **The first run said the opposite**: with one policy on both sides, `4 refused with RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`, and the lead looked refuted — but those came from the SERVER, whose ceiling counts the interval the name implies. `RpcChannelTransport.pair(policy:)` applies one policy to BOTH, so that run was ambiguous by construction: from the caller, a server refusing at its own ceiling is indistinguishable from a client doing so. Rebuilt with each side over a byte pipe carrying its own policy. **The two sides count different intervals under one field name**, and the both-sides run is now the control — it proves the mechanism works and the rig can provoke a refusal, so the zero is the client declining to count. **Not fixed**: releasing later is stream accounting on the client's hot path whose failure mode runs the WRONG way — too early admits too much, never refuses every subsequent call for the life of the connection — and the right release point per call shape is exactly what this round did not establish, since a locally cancelled call never receives a terminal frame. The cheap half, documenting which count is meant, is unclaimed. `P-157`
- **[519](519-nine-timers-before-the-deadline.md)** DEFERRED, rpc_dart — **confirmed in shape, refuted in its number, and the CONTROL holds the real finding.** A deadline costs `2` timers on unary (`4.0` against `2.0`) and `5` on a server stream (`14.1` against `9.1`) — not the three filed, and not a constant across shapes. **And a server-stream call arms NINE timers before any deadline exists**, which is larger than the thing the lead is about and which nobody has looked at. **The rig corrected itself once, instructively**: built with the endpoints OUTSIDE the counted zone it reported `1.0 timers/call` with the deadline apparently free — a subscription creates its timers in the zone it was registered in, so every RESPONDER timer landed in the root zone uncounted, and the responder is the half this lead is about. **A measurement that makes a claim vanish deserves the same suspicion as one that confirms it too easily.** Both secondary claims verified by reading: `disposerTimeout` is a mutable process-wide static, and every disposer is wrapped in `.timeout()`, so a synchronous one allocates a Timer to bound work that cannot block. **Not fixed, and the reason is round 518's**: "one deadline owner per call" spans three layers with different lifetimes on a hot path, and knowing the COUNT does not say which site is redundant — that needs the arming sites attributed, which this round did not build. The disposer half is separable and cheap; the nine-timer floor is unexplained. `P-156`

The lead's own note ("every shipped transport delivers whole frames") argues for the first. `P-155`
- **[517](517-the-drift-that-had-not-happened.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart — **the lead's evidence for acting now is refuted; its argument survives.** Three builders assemble request metadata and B-125 says they have already drifted, naming the case: with a null context the unary copy omits `x-request-id`. Read off the WIRE — two of the three are private, and what a peer receives is what the claim is about — all three produce the same header set, and unary carries `x-request-id` in exactly that row. The only differences are correct: `grpc-timeout` only when a context carried a deadline, `x-rpc-ping-timestamp` only on ping. **The rig's false start is the part worth keeping.** The transport's connection window-update is itself a metadata frame and goes FIRST, so the first version reported `x-rpc-conn-window-update` alone from all three arms — three identical rows, which is precisely what the true answer also looks like. **A negative that arrives that easily deserves a second look**, and the with-context rows are the control that would have caught it: `grpc-timeout` varying is what proves the rig can see a difference at all. - **[516](516-an-answer-that-read-as-an-incident.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **round 515 said take the tractable half first, and this is it.** One application NOT_FOUND produced `caller 2, responder 1` error records — three, for a server answering correctly — and the caller's two came from DIFFERENT sites, the non-OK trailer branch and the surrounding `catch`. Now `0 / 0`. The three sites logged on the SHAPE of the control flow rather than the MEANING of the status: a status is how a gRPC handler says things, so "the handler threw" and "something is broken" are different facts. `RpcStatus.isFault` names the second — UNKNOWN, INTERNAL, UNAVAILABLE, DATA_LOSS — and a status-less throw counts as a fault, since an unclassifiable failure is not an application answering. **Deliberately NARROWER than round 501's `_isServerHealthFailure`**, which also counts DEADLINE_EXCEEDED and RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED: a breaker asks "is this endpoint in trouble", this asks "did something break", and a slow server is not a broken one — a guard pins the difference so a later "unification" cannot start paging on a timeout. **The fault controls carry the round**: silencing every log would pass both witnesses perfectly, and INTERNAL plus a bare `StateError` are what stop that. **The counting method came from 515's failure** — `LogController.add`, not a `LogScope` subclass, because `child()` returns a plain scope and both endpoints derive one. - **[515](515-the-rig-never-reached-the-warning.md)** INCONCLUSIVE, rpc_dart — **three attempts, 10 000 hand-built no-op frames, ZERO warnings — and the claim is UNVERIFIED rather than refuted.** The frames never reach `_processResponderMessage`, not even its first branch (the not-started warning, tried with the endpoint deliberately unstarted), so something drops a metadata-only frame earlier and this round did not establish what.
