The cited worked example survives only because the flow controller is handed its scope directly. Override `LogController.add` instead: it runs before filtering, so it keeps the property the guidance wanted. **The lead's double-logging half should be taken first** — it needs no reachability argument, just one failing call and a count.
- **[514](514-the-drain-waited-for-a-tick.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **a drain whose job is to finish when the work does, waiting for a tick to notice.** Work that finished 1 ms in took `52 ms` to drain; now `5 ms`. **The control row earned the round an extra finding**: a handler that genuinely ran 120 ms still made the drain take `156 ms`, so the 50 ms poll added ~36 ms of rounding even when the work dominated — the cost was never confined to the pathological case. Fixed by signalling from `_cleanupStream` at count zero and awaiting with `.timeout()`, which is a Timer rather than a `DateTime.now()` comparison, so the wall-clock half lands in the same edit. **The signal sits BEFORE `_cleanupStream`'s early return** — it returns early when the id had no state, but what a drain waits for is the COUNT reaching zero, true either way. The two load-bearing guards cover the risky direction, a drain that returns too soon: a genuinely slow handler still waited for, and the budget still expiring and forcing cleanup — a completer that replaced the deadline instead of racing it would hang a deploy. **Bounds deliberately loose** (`< 35` against a measured 5), because the finding is an order of magnitude and B-196 exists about this gate's timing sensitivity. `drainUntilIdle` still polls — generic over a callback, nothing to be signalled by — so the lead's "two copies" remains, and the wall-clock claim is fixed on the circuit breaker's own documented argument rather than on a measurement, which the record says. `P-152`
- **[513](513-the-compression-that-grew-the-message.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **compression is for making messages smaller, and it was making them bigger.** An incompressible 32 B payload went `42 -> 62` bytes on the wire; the crossover sits between 192 B (+4) and 256 B (−14), gzip's fixed overhead being ~20 bytes. **The lead's second claim is REFUTED**: on `memoryPair` with `supportsZeroCopy: true` both sides, the response comes back `identity` — the responder does not gzip across the in-process boundary, though both header branches ARE gated on `!supportsZeroCopy` as filed. **The first version of the probe would have refuted the TRUE claim too**: it used `'a' * n` while testing whether compression makes messages BIGGER — the case least likely to grow — and duly reported a saving at 32 B. Testing growth needs incompressible input; the repeated rows survive as the control. **Fixed by comparing sizes rather than the threshold the sketch asks for**, and that control row is the argument: a 32 B repeated payload still compresses `42 -> 33`, which any threshold above 32 would throw away, and a threshold needs a number right for one kind of traffic and wrong for another. Safe because the frame's compression flag is per MESSAGE — `grpc-encoding: gzip` with an individual message sent plain is ordinary gRPC and the existing decoder already reads it. **The CPU half is untouched and comparing pays slightly more of it**, which the record says rather than implying the lead is fully discharged. `P-151`
- **[512](512-the-guard-that-was-not-a-bool-read.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **a guard whose whole purpose is to be cheaper than the thing it guards.** `CLAUDE.md` prescribes `if (_log.isInternal)` around every interpolating call and calls it "a bool read"; it walked every configured scope override with a `startsWith`. `235.5 ns` at twenty overrides against a real bool read's `1.8 ns` — **128x** — now `12.6 ns` and **flat**. The control is `LogScope.noop`, whose `isInternal` is a literal `false`: without it the 235 has no scale, and with it the record can say honestly that the fix reaches 7x a bool read rather than 1x. **Keyed by scope alone** — a tag override is an O(1) read taken before the cache, so a miss depends on scope only and tag changes need no invalidation. **`minLevel` drove the design**: a public mutable field with no setter to hook, so the cache remembers which level it was built under, one comparison instead of a correctness bug. **Two ablations, because the fix adds a risk the defect never had**: bypassing the cache returns the bench to `250 ns` and leaves all ten tests green — which is what established they are all GUARDs and corrected a mislabelled WITNESS group; removing the invalidation fails four, including `minLevel`. **Most deployments never paid this** (`_log = logger ?? LogScope.noop`), and the record says so rather than implying everyone was losing 235 ns. `P-150`
- **[511](511-forty-per-cent-of-a-call-is-entropy.md)** DEFERRED, rpc_dart — **about 40 us of every ~97 us unary call is OS entropy for two correlation ids.** `97.53 / 101.09 / 101.21` with the secure RNG against `52.77 / 58.01 / 57.51` with the fallback: a ~2x gap against a ~15% spread, with no overlap. **The end-to-end ablation is what makes it trustworthy** — a microbench of `Random.secure()` invites the objection that it is not measuring what a call pays, so `_strongRng` was forced to null in place (the path the library already takes on node) and the call re-timed. An earlier round already cut the draws 12 → 3 and hoisted the generator to a static, and its comment says `Random.secure().nextInt()` reaches the system entropy source on every call — what it never revisited is whether the generator should be SECURE at all. **`lib/` is untouched: the fix is one line and the codebase has already written its own justification** (`_strongRng`'s doc calls these ids "correlation in logs and on the wire, never secrets", and notes node already uses the weak generator for every token), but it changes what a VM deployment gets today, and B-111/B-116 set the precedent that a documented security posture is the owner's with the number in hand. **The context half is barely measured and the record says so** — a 6-link chain is ~35 us but SYNTHETIC, and an earlier draft of the probe wrongly quoted it as "37.8% of a call", double-counting against a figure it was never measured inside. `P-149`
- **[510](510-one-call-answered-three-times.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **a COST lead, and the count was hiding a defect.** One call to an unregistered method sent `grpc-status 12` end-of-stream **three times, all on stream `s3`** — a protocol violation anywhere the peer keeps stream state, and it triples the cost of the cheapest hostile input there is. **Printing what each frame IS rather than counting is what found it**: a count of 4 on that row is unremarkable beside the success row's 4. The responder has three `binding == null` sites (metadata frame, data frame, half-close), each firing `_sendGrpcErrorAndCleanup` through `_detached`. That helper marks the id SYNCHRONOUSLY — an earlier round's fix, whose comment calls it "the whole fix" — but the guard reading the mark also required `_respStreams[id] == null`, and the teardown runs in the detached `finally`. **The mark was made synchronous and the reader was left asynchronous.** Gating on set membership alone fixes it; both other outcomes are byte-for-byte unchanged. Two guards exist because the fix REMOVES a condition, which is the risky direction — id reuse across six calls, and a refused call not poisoning the next on that id, which would hang to its deadline. **The lead's own arithmetic is also wrong**: a success is 7 frames across both directions, not 5, and 5 are metadata, not 3. What remains — folding initial headers into the first response, Trailers-Only, a binary header encoding — is protocol and the owner's. `P-148`
- **[509](509-the-wrapper-around-an-empty-list.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **round 505 fixed the work INSIDE the loop and left the loop.** The scalar middleware helpers got an `isEmpty` early return there; the `async*` stream wrappers still iterated an empty list once per message, hop and allocation included. Returning the source unchanged when there is no middleware is worth ~1.03 us/message, 19%. **It changes a contract and the round states it as a test rather than letting it be found**: the middleware set is now fixed when the stream is BUILT, so one added mid-stream does not join the call — which is what interceptors already do, and round 505 had explicitly recorded the per-message re-read as *preserved, not decided*. **Seven run sets were needed to say any of this.** Two read `4.710` against `5.763` medians and looked settled; the next fixed set's median was `5.949`, which would have reversed the conclusion. The minima (`5.647/5.582/5.456` against `4.438/4.564/4.419/4.520`) never overlap, and minima are the right statistic because noise on this machine only ever adds time. The bench was reused rather than rebuilt — P-147 registers P-146's file under a new number. The other layers the lead names are untouched and are where the remaining ~4.4 us lives. `P-147`
- **[508](508-work-added-to-undo-work.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **`startCallerListening`'s own doc says the quiet part: "this subscription exists to keep the buffer drained".** Every inbound message was dispatched twice — routed to its stream's controller AND added to the transport-wide broadcast — so a caller-only endpoint had to attach a listener that does nothing except release what it should never have been charged. **The lead states its two claims in the wrong order and the measurement says so**: with the observer detached, after a server stream was FULLY consumed, a late subscriber was replayed `1001` for 1000 delivered — now `0`. The dispatch cost it leads with is ~6% (`median 5.987 -> 5.604 us/message`), a rounding error next to ~6 us per message. **A first single-run comparison read 16% and a second arm in the same run moved the wrong way**; printing the five-run distribution is what corrected it, and per-message cost is not flat across counts (75.8 / 17.9 / 7.07 us), which says only the largest arm measures a per-message cost at all. Fixed by skipping the broadcast for locally-initiated routed streams, by id parity; errors go through `addError` and are untouched, so the subscription is still needed for its error half. The sharpest guard reads the RESPONDER's broadcast off the transport and asserts the ids are odd — invert the parity and every server stops discovering calls, which a "the call succeeded" guard would not catch. **`test:web` is red and it is not this change**: the web-worker suite fails at the LOAD stage for the second round running, and rather than absorb that a third time it is filed as B-196. `P-146`
- **[507](507-the-copy-that-bought-nothing.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **every inbound chunk was copied into the reassembly buffer, including when the buffer was empty and the chunk already held whole frames.** The buffer exists for frames split across chunks; on a message-aligned transport that case does not arise, so the copy went in and straight back out as views. `1 MiB, receiver copies the payload out: 389.76 -> 191.45 us`, a 2.0x. **The honest number is 2x, not 725x** — the `view only` row's `227.02 -> 0.31 us` is the copy disappearing, not throughput, because nothing touches a byte there. At 64 B nothing moves, as the shape predicts, and that row is a control. **The bench had to be rebuilt once**: the first version echoed through `RpcCallerEndpoint`, where the JSON codec dominates at 1 MiB and would have attributed the time to the wrong layer. **Every test in the new file passes under the ablation and the file says so** — the defect is a cost, so no test can witness it; all nine are guards, and they were mislabelled WITNESS until the ablation corrected it. **What it costs is a contract**: a payload is now a view into the transport's chunk, so an `IRpcChannel` must not reuse a delivered buffer — documented, satisfied by every in-repo channel, invisible to every gate, and left to the owner with a one-line revert available. **And no real transport is limited by this**: the old path sustained 2566 MiB/s where a link does one to two orders less. `test:web` went red once on a worker suite and the ablation run is what established it as a flake. `P-145`
- **[506](506-the-limit-that-waited-for-the-payload.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **the limit was correct, present, well-phrased, and one line too late.** `_decodeAt` checked `maxMetadataLen` BELOW `if (data.length < payloadStart + payloadLen) return null;` — the keep-buffering path — so it was only reachable once the payload had entirely arrived. A declared 10 MiB metadata frame fed in 64 KiB chunks was held in full, `10485769` bytes against a 64 KiB ceiling, and then told it was too large. Both of the check's inputs are 9-byte header fields; moved above, it refuses at **9 bytes**. **A test asking "is it refused" passes against this defect** — the limit fires in both worlds and only the peak differs, so the bench measures bytes accepted before the error, yielding a turn per chunk so the figure is the buffer and not the writer. **The control is the same 10 MiB without the metadata flag**, legal and accepted in full both times, which pins the cause to the classification and stops the small after-figure reading as a rig that cannot feed 10 MiB. **The sibling already had it right**: the client's `_refusedFrameHeader` applies the ceiling from the header and returns null when `closeOnOversizedFrame` is true, the server's default — half the codebase correct, which both confirmed the intent and said where to look. Single-chunk delivery (dart:io WebSocket) is a different defence, bounded by `_maxBufferedFrameBytes`, and untouched. `P-144`
- **[505](505-the-list-moved-under-the-call.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **what went stale across the await was the ITERATOR.** `for (final m in _middlewares)` awaits in its body; `close()` does `clear()` and `addMiddleware` appends, both public, and a Dart list iterator compares modification counts on every `moveNext()` — so an in-flight call got `ConcurrentModificationError`, a `StateError`, instead of its status. One middleware is enough: the `moveNext()` that ENDS the loop is the one that checks. `.reversed` is a lazy view of the same list, so the response half carried it identically. Fixed with `List.of` in both loops behind an `isEmpty` early return, keeping the common no-middleware call allocation-free. **The fix changes the error's TYPE, not whether the call fails** — `close()` still fails it, now as `RpcCancelledException: Endpoint closed` — so a round asking "did the call succeed" would have scored this as no change at all. **And the bench needed an EMPTY-list arm**: an empty loop never awaits, so it never observes the mutation, and without that arm "close() breaks an in-flight call" explains the table equally well and the fix gets aimed at `close()`. The interceptor list is cleared by the same `close()` and needs no fix — those loops build the chain synchronously, awaiting only inside the closures they create, which is why this fix is two lines and not six. `P-143`
- **[504](504-the-invariant-only-the-doc-enforced.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **the invariant was enforced by a sentence.** `rpcMethodPathFromKey` splits a binding key on the last dot and its doc has always stated what makes that correct — "a service name may contain them, a method name may not" — while `parseRpcMethodPath`, twenty lines away, applied ONE dot-admitting pattern to both halves. So `('a', 'b.c')` and `('a.b', 'c')` were distinct pairs with one key. **The lead called it low-likelihood and that is wrong**: registered `service "a.b", methods "c" and "secret"` is an ordinary package-qualified gRPC service with ordinary methods, and only the REQUEST is crafted — `/a/b.secret` reached `a.b/secret`, while `/a/c` and `/zzz/c` stayed UNIMPLEMENTED as the controls. Holding the registration fixed and varying the path is what showed it. Fixed by splitting the pattern in two. **The escalation this round started with is REFUTED and that is the most useful thing in it**: an interceptor denying service `a.b` denied BOTH paths and saw the resolved name twice, because the binding is resolved before the middleware context exists — no in-process auth bypass. **And a third measurement level was needed**, because the caller transport validates outbound metadata against the same policy, so the first after-table was the caller refusing itself and said nothing about a foreign peer; a hand-built frame straight into the channel reads `grpc-status 3` against the honest path's "accepted". - **[503](503-the-throw-that-left-half-a-service.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **`registerContract` mutated first and validated second, so every failure exit was a partial commit.** `setup()` throwing left `contracts [Svc] methods []`; a colliding key on the second method left `contracts [a, a.b] methods [a.b.c, a.b.ok]`, and `a.b/ok` then **answered `ok` from a contract whose registration threw**. **And the endpoint could not be repaired** — the lead only noted the retry gets "already registered"; what follows is that a registration failure is terminal for that service name, because catch-fix-register-again is the only recovery there is. Fixed by running `setup()` and reserving every key into a local `pending` map, then committing contract and methods in one step. **Why it survived: every existing test drives `registerContract` once, and successfully** — this is RPC-21 with a variant the lens had not named, drive it twice where the FIRST attempt failed. **The control is a clean contract registered twice**, also refused, without which "already registered" reads as the defect rather than as correct behaviour. The two failure routes are independent: `setup()` throwing needs nothing else wrong, while the method-loop route exists only through B-113's dotted-key ambiguity — which the probe's after-table still prints, since suppressing it would hide that dependency. `P-141`
- **[502](502-the-shape-that-was-never-admitted.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **the rate limiter charged in `handleData`, so a call that sent no messages was never charged at all.** `100 / 100` admitted against `global: max 5`, with no upper bound — the arm would read 10 000 of 10 000. A bidi subscription is exactly that shape (zero requests, server pushes), so subscriptions were unlimited on the component whose job is limiting them. Unary and server-stream charge at establishment; only the two shapes whose request stream can legitimately be empty did not. Fixed with `_check` before `next` plus `firstIsPrepaid: true`, cost `max(1, messages)`. **The first fix was additive and a control rejected it**: charging establishment ON TOP closed the hole equally well (100 → 5) and moved `CONTROL bidi, 1 request message` from 5 to 2 — every existing one-message config silently halved, to fix a case none of them hit. **Three ablations, because two halves fail independently**: drop the establishment charge and both witnesses read 100; make it additive and the witnesses PASS while the one-message guard reads 2, which is the only thing that can tell the two candidate fixes apart; never clear `prepaid` and nothing is charged or refused at all. **B-111's second half is NOT fixed and is the owner's** — `global: 5` + `perMethod: 1000` admits 100/100, and the class doc states that precedence in words, so it is a documented decision rather than an oversight. `P-140`
- **[501](501-the-fallback-nobody-chose.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **`failureOn == null ||` is the right default for a filter and the wrong one for a classifier**, and reading the two interceptors side by side is what showed it: the retry interceptor's default is chosen, documented and narrow, the breaker's was *everything except cancellation* — not a decision, just what the `||` does when the field is absent. Five NOT_FOUND answers from a server doing its job opened the breaker, and because state is per interceptor instance and not per method, an unrelated healthy method then answered `BREAKER OPEN` for 30 s. Fixed with a named `_isServerHealthFailure` — UNAVAILABLE, RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED, INTERNAL, UNKNOWN, DEADLINE_EXCEEDED, plus anything carrying no status at all. **Deliberately WIDER than the sibling's set, which is the point of the comparison**: a breaker asks "is this endpoint in trouble", a retry asks "is another attempt worth making"; copying the neighbour would have been the wrong conclusion from the right observation. Canary: `return true;` restored, exactly the 5 witnesses fail and all 7 guards hold. **Why it survived every existing test: all of them supply `failureOn` explicitly**, so nothing ever exercised the fallback. **And two of the round's own guard arms were wrong first** — an unregistered method does not time out, it answers `UNIMPLEMENTED` immediately, so that arm became a fifth witness and the real no-status arm needed a codec that throws. - **[500](500-ask-the-primitive-first.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart — **B-109's mechanism belongs to the LANGUAGE, and the language does not do it.** The lead claims cancelling a `Future.asStream()` subscription leaves the callback attached; twelve lines with no library code in them: `CANCELLED -> 0 callbacks fired`, `left open -> 100` (the control that makes the zero admissible). Refuted at the primitive, so nothing accumulates however long a token lives. **Then a census anyway, because the primitive is half the answer** — a site observing the token with a bare `.then(` really would retain its callback, which is the true version of the concern. Six observers, all `asStream().listen` with a stored subscription, all cancelling it; `cancelled.then(` appears nowhere in `lib/`. The sixth is `ping.dart:244`, added by round 499 one round earlier, so the census checked its own predecessor. The sketch's `addListener`/`removeListener` API is now a change with no defect behind it — still the clearer contract, but the owner's call. **A second instalment on a lesson already bought**: the round's level-2 arm reached for RSS across 20 000 calls and got `+24 MiB` against `-25 MiB` with the signs flipping between runs; P-128 had already established that. `checked/C-58`, `P-138`
- **[499](499-the-keepalive-hung-on-the-case-it-exists-for.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **`ping()` pre-checked its context once and then ignored it, so the keepalive hung on exactly the connection it exists to detect.** Against a peer that accepts the ping and answers nothing: `context deadline 200ms -> 3003ms` and `token cancelled at 200ms -> 3002ms`, both the PROBE's budget rather than a library bound, while the explicit `timeout: 200ms` worked at `222ms`. Now `204ms` and `203ms` (the latter as `RpcCancelledException`). **A deadline SENT is not a deadline ENFORCED** — it went out as `grpc-timeout` and bounded the server, so everything about the call said 200 ms except the code that waits. RPC-14 in its mirror: not what a fired timeout leaves running, but a wait that could not fire — and on a health check the failure and the detector share a code path, so the detector fails exactly when needed. Three halves, three canaries, each failing on its own witness. **The clock witness is the one to reuse**: a wall-clock step cannot be caused in a test, but `sentAt` is a constructor parameter, so injecting an hour in the past stands in for it and the ablation reads `1:00:00.012557`. Two GUARDs keep it honest — a peer that answers stays fast, and a ping with NO bound asked for still waits (inventing one would answer B-107 by the back door). `.ignore()` ships reasoned but unwitnessed, and the record says so. `P-137`
- **[498](498-giving-up-without-telling-anyone.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **a caller that gave up on a timeout told the server nothing, so the handler ran on for a caller already gone.** With no deadline: `unary 60.0s`, `clientStream 60.0s`, `serverStream 65.0s` (the PROBE's budget — nothing bounds it), `grpc-timeout=[null]` and `cancelled=0` throughout; against a 500 ms-deadline control where every piece works (`grpc-timeout` sent, `RpcDeadlineExceededException`, `cancelled=1`). The lead SPLITS: the abandonment is a defect and is fixed (`cancelled 0 -> 1`), the implicit 60 s is a policy question left open, because removing or extending it changes behaviour for every existing caller. RPC-25 with the duty already named in a sibling's doc — `notifyPeerOfAbort`'s comment says `_sendCancellationToServer` "is reachable only through a cancellation token", which is a list with one entry and an invitation to find the others; timeout was another. - **[467](467-a-refusal-that-answered-twice.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-90 closed, and its second question was the whole answer.** A call opened as a peer opens one — metadata, then payload — is TWO frames, and both of `responder_pipeline`'s refusals answered both: `draining [14, 14]`, `ceiling at 1 [8, 8]`. Also answered its question 1 — the CEILING refusal does it too and had never been driven; its arm needs the client and responder to hold SEPARATE policies, since round 463 made the caller's own ceiling refuse first (C-29's test note, four rounds late). - **[460](460-one-terminal-message-not-two.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart_http — **B-86's last half, and it CLOSES the lead.** A 200 with no `grpc-status` anywhere is reachable — the synthesised status covers non-2xx only, so the trailer set comes out empty — and the consumer is told: `status=14 "The stream closed before the peer sent a status"`, against a `grpc-status: 0` control returning cleanly on the same raw server and a `grpc-status: 5` arm reported as itself, so the refusal is neither the harness nor a catch-all. **The difference from http2 is the NUMBER of terminal messages, not the empty metadata the lead reasoned from** — http2 emitted two, and the first closed the consumer before the second could carry a status; HTTP/1.1 emits exactly one, so there is no ordering to lose. The residency charge is a different thing: `bufferedBytes` is a property of `RpcTransportMessage` and the charge is in CORE's `responder_pipeline`, which every transport feeds, so **a transport with nothing of its own to charge is the correct shape**. And the third read is what makes the second's absence right rather than tolerable: **the window that charge protects cannot open on HTTP/2**, because the method is a `:path` pseudo-header on the frame that opens the stream — payload-before-method is a channel-transport shape. `bench: none` with the reason in the frontmatter: the claim is about where a charge LIVES, so the evidence is a call graph. **Left standing deliberately**: http2 passes the raw nullable where core passes the effective value, agreeing only via the parser's repeated fallback — stating it would be a change no canary could kill, which is round 366's precedent, so it is recorded not shipped. Exactly the failure `frame_multiplexed_channel`'s own comment describes. One accessor now, `maxFramedMessageBytes`, shared with the frame channel which had it inline. **Bound on the framed size, REPORT the configured one** — an existing test caught that distinction, because naming `max + 5` names a number the operator never set. Canary `Expected: 'ok' / Actual: 'status=8'`. Left deliberately: `parser.dart:113`, the same arithmetic answering a buffer question rather than a frame one. **http2 untouched and unmeasured** (B-79 is that lead). **And the workspace gate is flaking under load, with names**: four runs, four different tests, all wall-clock families, at load 7.5-15.4 after hours of back-to-back suites — one of them (`caller_response_limit`) was a REAL failure of this round and is fixed; the rest pass alone, and `rpc_dart_isolate` passes 91/91 through `melos exec`. - **[457](457-my-own-premise-was-false.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http2 — **B-91 was not pre-existing: round 455 caused it, and this round is the correction.** Question 1 in process: the codec is fine (`4096 -> 43 -> 4096`, IDENTICAL, both spellings); over a channel pair every size works. So http2-specific — where 455 had changed something. The read that settles it, `parser.dart:212-218`: with NO decompressor, which is exactly what the http2 transports build, the parser **cannot** de-frame a COMPRESSED message, so it re-frames the compressed payload itself and emits a complete frame deliberately. **455's premise — "both call sites pass parser output, which is de-framed by construction" — is false in that one branch, and it is the branch http2 always takes.** Framing it again wrapped an outer `compressed: false` header round it and the codec met gzip bytes; `wireStatusFor` redacted the throw, which is why B-91 looked undiagnosable. Heuristic restored, with a comment saying why it is load-bearing. Canary: 455's framing against the new guard reads `Expected: 'saw:yyyy…' / Actual: 'status=13'`, identity arms green. **B-78 RE-OPENED** — the right fix is for the parser to say which branch it took, a core API change, not improvised under time pressure. **Two tests 455 wrote asserted the wrong contract and are inverted**, one now characterising B-78's hole so a proper fix breaks it deliberately (and its FILENAME is wrong, since `mv` is outside the allowlist). **What let it ship, which is the durable part: nothing in 249 http2 tests exercised a genuinely compressed message**, so a green suite twice over could not see it — that arm now exists. Reading error worth keeping: `result.add(payload)` was quoted as evidence about the variable without reading the lines above that reassign it. One unnamed websocket flake under full-workspace load, green in isolation and on re-run
- **[456](456-the-registry-was-lenient-and-nothing-else-was.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-82, and the lead had the direction backwards.** It expected a hand-built peer, because rpc_dart's own caller always spells `identity` lower-case; but the encoding is selected by a header on the call CONTEXT (`_context?.getHeader(grpcEncoding)`), so a USER picks the spelling: `identity -> OK saw:x` against **`Identity -> status=13 Internal server error`**. From a foreign peer at compression flag 0 the same value is INERT, which is the opposite of the prediction. Mechanism is three answers to one question, two of them normalising: `isSupported('Identity')` true so nothing is refused, `'Identity' != 'identity'` true so compression switches ON, `compress` normalises and changes nothing — the compressed FLAG goes out on bytes nothing compressed. One accessor, `isIdentity`, now at all eleven sites, absorbing the `== null` half too; inbound metadata is not rewritten, as decided. **The canary is why the witness changed packages**: over a channel pair the end-to-end arm passed on BOTH sides, because in one process compress and decompress both normalise — a test that passes either way proves nothing, so the witness is in http2 and the core test is labelled a contract test. Removing the `!= null` guards also removed seven null PROMOTIONS, which the analyzer caught rather than reading. **Filed, not folded**: `gzip` and `GZIP` both answer `status=13` before and after, so it is not case — B-91, with the caution not to call it "compression is broken" until an in-process arm says which side fails. Gate green
- **[455](455-the-guess-over-bytes-the-peer-chose.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http2 — **B-78: `ensureGrpcFrame`'s heuristic was reachable from an ordinary application message, and turned a 13-byte one into an 8-byte one silently.** Reading settled the shape before any bench and is what made the arm constructible: the function is called on `RpcMessageParser`'s output at BOTH sites, and the parser emits de-framed BODIES (`result.add(payload)`) — so the bytes it inspects are an application body the peer chose. Measured end to end over a raw HTTP/2 socket, same body LENGTH in both arms and one byte different: `first byte 0x00 -> payload 13B UNCHANGED` against `0x99 -> 18B re-framed`; in the first the layer above read five of the body's own bytes as a header. **The fix is smaller than the decision's B-62 shape** — there was no fact to carry, because it is a CONSTANT: both call sites pass parser output, so `frameParsedMessage` frames unconditionally. Renamed deliberately, since "ensure" is what invites the check back, and the function is package-internal so it costs no public surface. Canary `Expected: <18> / Actual: <13>`, `+1 -1`. **Four test files referenced the old name and split two ways** (canary.md item 9 in practice): two used it as a framing helper and only needed renaming; `grpc_wire_compliance_test.dart`'s case named *"does not double-wrap"* was PINNING the defect and is rewritten to the new contract with the measurement in its comment. Left alone and stated: the sibling `isGrpcFrame`, the same heuristic as a bool, with no callers in `lib/`. Gate green
- **[454](454-the-order-the-sibling-wrote-down.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-83: the drain refusal's order was the bug the ceiling's comment exists to prevent, not a specialisation.** A late metadata-only frame on a COMPLETED stream, arriving while draining, was answered `status=14 "Server is shutting down"` — a SECOND terminal status on a stream the peer had already been told was OK — against `NONE (ignored)` for the same frame when not draining, so the ordering is what produced it. The check now sits with the ceiling refusal, after the closed-stream guard, whose comment already stated the rule: *"a late frame for a torn-down id cannot burn a slot"*. Canary `Expected: empty / Actual: ['14']`, `+2 -1`. **The GUARD arm is load-bearing, not decoration** — moving a refusal later is exactly the change that can disable it, so the third test opens a genuinely NEW stream while draining and requires UNAVAILABLE; a drain that stops refusing new calls is not a drain, and nothing else in the suite covered that. **And it found a second defect**: that new stream is refused TWICE, `status=14, status=14`, one refusal per frame, because the refusal does not record the id it refused. A literal written back in — `readInt('maxHeaders', 64)` — fails as `at location ['maxHeaders'] is <64> instead of <128>`, the field-by-field map naming the drifted field rather than failing an opaque `==`. `+2 -1`, and **the split is informative**: only the EMPTY-MAP arm catches fallback drift, because the round-trip arms have `toMap` write the value and `fromMap` read it instead of falling back — the three tests cover different journeys, not the same one three times. Constants are PRIVATE: the lint wanted a doc comment on each, and 13 documented public members is a real addition to a published surface for something no caller names. **B-85 does NOT close** — the JS shim carried as strings in both Swift and Kotlin needs `analyze:native` and `test:wasm:device` on both platforms, so it stays with B-38 and B-03 as owner work. - **[452](452-the-weakest-lead-had-a-leak-in-it.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http2 — **B-84, "the weakest of B-70's remainder", had a real leak in it.** `resetStream` — the path a CANCELLATION takes — left the stream's outgoing pump behind: `pumps 1 -> 1` against `releaseStreamId`'s `1 -> 0` on the same stream shape. `releaseStreamId`'s own comment states the rule it was missing: *"Release anything parked on the server's window first, or a caller awaiting sendMessage never unwinds once its stream is gone."* **The lead's arithmetic was short** — it names `_fcForget` as what `releaseStreamId` adds, and it adds TWO things; the other, `_outgoingPumps.dispose()`, is the one that leaked. **And neither of its questions could be ASKED as filed**: those maps were private with no getter, which is how a lead about them sat unmeasured for 26 rounds inside B-70. Three counts added to `_buildHealthDetails` is the instrument, and RPC-15's round-396 note is exactly this — extend the instrument, one line, pays forward. Canary `Expected: <0> / Actual: <1>`, `+1 -1`, control green; the test asserts the BEFORE value too, because an arm with no pump to lose would pass silently. **Not confirmed and said so**: the HANG the comment promises — a 256 KiB send against the 65535-byte window did not park, so the leak is STATE not a stall; `fcOutstanding` reads 0 in every row so `_fcForget` was added on the sibling's shape rather than a measurement; and the third block was not armed. - **[451](451-the-disposer-was-in-the-other-layer.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart — **B-73: the responder DOES bound its deadline, one LAYER up from where the lead looked.** `_ensureResponderContext` arms a timer from `context.deadline` and `_onDeadlineExceeded` cancels the handler's token, then arms a RECLAIM backstop for a handler that ignores it — which `CallProcessor`, the half that HAS the disposer, does not have; so the asymmetry runs opposite to the lead's reading. Its three questions answered in order: torn down YES, by the pipeline not the processor, and the consumer sees an ERROR not a clean end — the third was the gate, which is why this closes as a negative (C-50) rather than a fix. `3 items / DeadlineExceeded / responder timer fired` twice, against a no-deadline control of `100 items / CLEAN END / not fired`. **The isolating observable is a responder-side LOG record and that is the reusable part**: one `grpc-timeout` header arms a timer on BOTH ends, so the caller's exception is equally consistent with the responder doing nothing, and only `_onDeadlineExceeded` emits its notice. **Two probe builds lost to L-15 first** — a hand-driven responder read `payloads=0` in the timeout row AND its control, so the handler had never run; recorded in C-50 rather than debugged, because the log observable made the arm unnecessary. - **[449](449-the-window-was-real-the-loss-was-not.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart_http2 — **B-76's central claim refuted, and the owner's step one must not be carried out.** The lead says http2 accepts and silently drops sends for the duration of `await factory()`, because `_disconnected` is set only in the catch. **The window is real** — `health()` read INSIDE it says `disconnected=false`, which is what makes this a refutation rather than an untested claim — **and nothing is lost in it**: the old connection is discarded before the await, so the send answers `RpcStatusException code=14`, with controls on both sides of the window reading ACCEPTED. The proxy cannot have the defect at all: `_inner` is nulled before any factory runs and `_require()` throws on null, so its guard is an ABSENCE rather than a boolean whose timing could be wrong. Negative filed as **C-48**. **Not simply good news, and this is what B-76 is now for**: the three machines tell a caller three different things about one state, and http2 disagrees with itself by timing — UNAVAILABLE during the await, FAILED_PRECONDITION after, beneath a comment saying the code matches the websocket sibling *deliberately* so that one `catch` covers both. One says *call reconnect()*, the other *retry*, so a caller's strategy depends on how far into a reconnect its send landed. B-76 re-scoped, not closed; the websocket arm was READ, not benched, and finishing that table is the next round. No source changed
- **[448](448-the-promise-that-was-too-big.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-81: cancelling an in-flight unary call NEVER SETTLED on a transport whose end-of-stream send never completes**, while the streaming sibling returned `RpcCancelledException` over the same transport (P-102, and that sibling arm is what makes the reading about ordering rather than about the transport). Two corrections to the lead: the reading is the CALL's future, not `cancel()` — the `await` sits in the call's own `finally` — and **"never throws" is literally TRUE**, because `_notifyPeerOfCancellation` is ONE shared function wrapping both its branches in try/catch. That is why it read as a closed door; the defect is that a catch cannot catch a hang. **Both comments were right about their own concern, and the unary one solved its concern with the wrong instrument**: the real ordering is notice-before-ID-RELEASE (the notice is a frame on that id, so releasing first lands it on whatever call next holds the number), and awaiting it in `finally` promoted that into notice-before-RETURN. Fixed by chaining the release onto the notice instead of blocking on it, bounded at 5 s so a wedged send cannot hold the id for the life of the process. Canary: `Expected: 'RpcCancelledException' / Actual: 'hung'`, `+2 -1`, with an id-release GUARD that polls `activeStreams` back to 0 rather than sleeping once. **Not fixed, and stated**: no shipping transport was shown to hang this way — the wrapper is built for the bench, and `base_processor`'s claim about platform-reply sends stays unverified; also round 445 widened the window, since `sendMetadata(endStream: true)` now waits for a credit-parked send. Canary: `Expected: 'status 14 after 2' / Actual: 'clean end after 2'`, `+2 -1` with 429's witness and the conforming guard both green. **The owner's decision was refuted by measurement and is not what shipped**: it said to put the check in CORE for every transport, on the reasoning that this would cover the unproven HTTP/1.1 half for free — but core already raises on every ending it can see (three variants over `RpcChannelTransport`, status-trailer control reading NO ERROR), so the gap was http2-local and **the free coverage never existed**. - **[446](446-the-knob-that-only-turned-down.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-72: `RpcContext` held four private header limits equal to `RpcSecurityPolicy`'s defaults and reachable from no policy, so the knob was monotone downward only.** Measured with P-100, which varies the KNOB and holds the input fixed: raised to 512 the context still kept 128, and the end-to-end call **SUCCEEDED with 73 of 200 headers missing** — no error, no log, on either side. The equality with the defaults is what hid it. Size now belongs to the policy alone, which throws; what the context still drops is structural only (empty, pseudo-header, off-pattern, CR/LF/NUL), which bounds nothing. **Two canaries, one per half** — the count/bytes ceiling restored reads `Expected: <200> Actual: <127>`, the name/value pair restored reads `Expected: <2> Actual: <0>` — and **one of the three new assertions is explicitly NOT a witness**: "past the ceiling the caller is refused" passes with the ceiling restored too, because 128 kept plus the system headers still exceeds 128, so it raises for a different reason. The canary is what found that out. **The expensive part: four tests went red and they were not stale.** `f876d602` had deliberately made these caps effective across the builder and `merge`, stating its trade — *"a confusing ArgumentError at send time instead of a bounded context at build time"*. That sentence was RE-MEASURED before reversing it (L-13): the send-time symptom is now `RpcMetadataViolation: Too many metadata headers: 205 > 128`, a type introduced after `f876d602`, which removed its premise while the decision resting on it went unread. The test file was rewritten rather than deleted, keeping what it was really protecting (builder and one-shot agree, `merge` keeps the union, an add cannot evict). **Found by running the suite, not by looking**: the pre-fix grep covered `test/core/` and the file is in `test/contracts/` — L-12 on the axis of DIRECTORY. Both of this round's files pass alone under `-p node`; together they hit 445's `ENETDOWN`. Gate otherwise green

So the scope was counted BEFORE the fix (L-12) and all three were measured. **`sendDirectObject(endStream: true)` needed no race at all** — a direct object never calls `tryConsume`, so nothing made it wait: `[data:600, direct, end, data:16]`, the end reaching the peer ahead of a frame the sender was still waiting to place, which is round 366's field failure on a third path. So a context carrying `content-type`, which is what forwarding an inbound HTTP header map produces and the reserved-set doc names as the case, **failed the keepalive INVALID_ARGUMENT while every other call shape on that same context succeeded**. So: correctness on the keepalive path, not a flow-control hole. **The class already had a witness and it did not cover this site**: `cancellation_header_reserved_test.dart` has a case literally named *"the control keys never reach the wire, ordinary ones do"*, written over the unary and streaming shapes when the keys were reserved. The owner accepted that. **What makes closing safe rather than abandonment**: the tripwire was RE-RUN first — `send_after_raw_socket_close_test.dart`, 4 tests green — and it lives in the ordinary suite, not under `.dart_tool/probe/`, so it runs on every `test:unit` without anyone remembering it exists. The day `package:web_socket_channel` changes, it goes red and the question comes back on its own. **A lead closed with its detector left in the gate can re-open itself.** The defect is real and remains unfixed; options 2 (ship as defence in depth) and 3 (upstream) are written into the lead as declined, not forgotten. - **[442](442-the-doc-that-said-the-opposite.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **B-71's gap could not be witnessed, and looking for the witness found something worse: the shipped docs assert the gap does not exist.** The lead records, measured, that *"a browser client on a half-open path has no liveness signal at all"*. The public doc for the same parameter said *"**A web client is not unprotected**, but it cannot be tuned here"*, and the stub said it harder — *"is not left unprotected — the browser is doing it"*. **Both false.** A browser does run ping/pong, which is what the sentence rests on, but it exposes neither the interval nor the OUTCOME: a missing pong never reaches the page and does not close the socket the way `dart:io` does. True of the frames, false of the only thing the frames are for. **Two copies, in the API doc and the implementation, each reading as corroboration of the other** — which is how it survived since round 422. Both now carry the lead's table and the consequence neither had: on the web, set a deadline on every call, it is the only bound there is. One code change: `pingInterval` added to the stub's discard tuple, whose own comment says it exists "so a reader sees the platform cannot honour them" — the one parameter whose absence costs something was the one missing from it. **The witness is impossible with this harness and the reason is recorded** so nobody re-attempts it: `dart:io`'s WebSocket auto-pongs at the protocol layer, hand-rolling the upgrade needs a SHA-1 this package does not depend on, and `test:web` is node not a cuttable browser. B-71 does NOT close — its three fix shapes all need the owner, and option 1 (document it) is now spent. **B-30 closed on the owner's instruction.** Gate green incl. `test:web`

The damage has a signature — an ASCII sentence followed by non-ASCII on one comment line, `grep -nE '// [A-Za-z][A-Za-z ,.()]*[а-яА-ЯёЁ]'` — and over every tracked `*.dart` it returns **no damage**: every hit is a pre-existing Russian sentence containing an English identifier (`IBlobClient реализация`, `StreamController с onCancel`), all in untouched `lib/`/`example/`. **So 439's six were the whole population and its repair was complete** — which "I fixed the ones I saw" could not have established. **The rule is also now costed**: `replace_all` used eight times, each checked first against a `sort | uniq -c` of the file's comment lines, two rejected as prefixes and done singly; the check takes about as long as reading them. 226 tests pass. `test/` is down to 443 lines across 21 files — **a third of round 435's 1338**, and 21 of those are C-47 fixtures that never go away. No stale number-comment in 171 lines: three rounds at zero against 437/438's two, so that pair stand but the rate does not generalise. Gate green
- **[439](439-the-replace-that-matched-a-prefix.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-30's `test/endpoint/` done, 187 Cyrillic lines to 0, and the round injected a defect its own detector caught by luck.** `// Регистрируем сервис` appeared four times so the sweep batched it — and it is a PREFIX of six longer comments, each of which came out half-translated (`// Register the service. первый раз`). **It compiles, every test passes, `analyze` is clean: nothing in a Dart gate can see a mangled comment.** What caught it was the round's own Cyrillic grep, working only because the surviving tail happened to be Russian — `// Регистрируем сервис v2` would have become `// Register the service. v2` and passed the sweep, the gate AND the detector. Fourth failure shape for C-47's table and the worst-behaved: the others fail loudly or are at least still visible to the detector. **Rule: anchor a bulk comment replace to the end of the line, or do the sites one at a time** — identical comments batch safely, comments that merely BEGIN the same way do not, and nothing distinguishes them when you write the edit. Also: the 437/438 stale-comment rate did NOT continue — 187 lines, zero number-comments disagreeing with their code, so the earlier pair were real but the rate is not uniform. `test/` is now 614 lines across 27 files, under half what it was at round 435. Gate green
- **[438](438-the-comment-that-was-right-once.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-30's `rpc_context` family, 170 Cyrillic lines to 1, and 437's stale comment turns out to be a RATE.** Round 437 argued that prose nobody reads is prose nobody checks; this round swept a comparable volume as a test of that and found another in the second file it opened — `// Отменяем через 100мс` on `Timer(Duration(milliseconds: 1))`. **`git log -S` makes it the more instructive case: the timer WAS 100 ms when the comment was written and became 1 in a later commit.** It was not wrong when written; it DRIFTED, and the drift was invisible because a reviewer skipping a language they do not read cannot notice that the number beside it moved. Two in two rounds, both stating a number checkable against the line below in one second. 305 tests pass. **A 13th fixture for C-47** — `RpcContext.withHeaders({'x-name': 'тест с unicode 🚀'})` asserted to throw `ArgumentError`, so translating it inverts the test — and **a third failure shape for C-47's canary table**: prose data with the producer 350 lines from the assertion (`'Aggregated N элементов'` at `:592`, `contains('3 элементов')` at `:231`/`:271`). Three sites, one edit; a PARTIAL edit is what breaks it. Bears on the deferred half: the 277 `lib/` comment lines have had the same exemption from review, on code that ships, and nobody has counted how many are wrong. Gate green
- **[437](437-the-lint-that-mandates-the-ambiguous-form.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-30's `test/serializers/`, the slice only C-47 made sweepable**, because three of its five files mix prose to translate with fixtures that must not be, on adjacent lines. 85 Cyrillic lines to 10; counting every script, the directory now holds **20 non-ASCII lines and every one is in C-47** — a falsifiable prediction that came out exact, so the census missed nothing. 126 tests pass. **Translating a comment is what subjects it to review**: `// < 3ms среднее время` sat on `lessThan(10000)`, which is 10 ms, and had been wrong since it was written — the sibling one line down was right, so this was an unchecked slip rather than a systematic one. A reviewer who skips a language they do not read skips the CLAIM inside it. **CANARY — the repo's own lint mandates the construct that broke round 435.** `$minTimeμs` reads as one identifier and is not (`μ` is not ASCII, so it ends the name and `μs` is a literal); the obvious repair `${minTime}μs` is refused by `unnecessary_brace_in_string_interps` under `--fatal-infos`. **The analyzer knows the boundary from the grammar and therefore calls the braces redundant — removing the only signal a human reader has.** Reverted with a comment; the lint config is the owner's to change. `lib/` still deferred pending the owner's answer to 436. Gate green
- **[436](436-the-detector-that-cannot-see-its-own-class.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-30 into core: `test/zero_copy/` swept, and the repo's whole fixture class censused into C-47.** 3 files, 171 Cyrillic + 98 emoji to zero, which takes the repo's remaining emoji from 154 lines to 56 — every one of those 98 was decoration, in a test name, a comment or a `print`. 29 tests run, not merely analysed. **Two findings about the detector B-30 names as "both the detector and the check".** It cannot tell prose from data: `cbor_test.dart` pins `'привет'` and `'☺'` to their exact hex (`6cd0bf…`, `63e298ba`), so translating the string changes the expected encoding. And it is **SCRIPT-SPECIFIC, so it cannot enumerate the class it keeps hitting** — `[а-яА-Я]` flags `optimized_cbor_test.dart:167` only because `'Hello 🌍 Мир 世界'` happens to contain `Мир`, while `'你好世界'` and `'مرحبا بالعالم'` two lines above in the SAME map literal are invisible to it, as is `'世界' * 5000` in the sibling file, 10,000 characters and the largest unicode fixture in the repo. **The split also changes shape between packages**: per FILE in transports (3 of 8), per LINE in core, where one file holds comments to translate at `:148` and fixtures not to at `:135` — so the remaining 26 core files cannot be driven from `grep -rl` at all. `test:web` run because the swept file carries a dart2js constraint in a comment, the kind most easily lost in translation. **`lib/` stays deferred** — the owner's decision is an ORDER and in-scope work remains; round 435's recommendation to take the 39 runtime log lines is put to the owner rather than acted on (L-13). - **[435](435-the-half-that-ships.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_isolate + rpc_dart_http2 — **B-30's transport test half, and a THIRD axis the lead never separated.** Five files translated, transports now at 3 Cyrillic files from 8, and 31 tests re-RUN under renamed test names rather than merely analysed. **The three that remain must keep their Cyrillic**: `'Ошибка'` asserted to percent-encode to ASCII, `'кириллица' * 6` proving a close reason is measured in BYTES, and the wasm UTF-8 round-trip — in all three the non-ASCII-ness IS the subject, so the lead's claim that the grep is *"both the detector and the check"* would demand breaking them. That grep also returns **10 gitignored Android resource artifacts** in nine languages nobody wrote; it has to be `git ls-files`-scoped. **The recount is the finding**: the lead defers `lib/` because *"16 of its files are `rpc_data`'s ... PUBLISHED API surface that dartdoc renders"*, but the weight is one file it never names — `rpc_notify/lib/src/stream_distributor.dart`, **176 lines, more than any test file in the repo, and 39 of them runtime LOG messages**. Comment and log are different axes: a comment is read by whoever opens the source, a log is EMITTED into the user's own stream and cannot be turned off without turning the logger off. Emoji, counted the same way, never reach `lib/` at all — the two halves of one style rule have different distributions. Canary: the sweep's own `μ` bug, which passes grep and review and fails `dart analyze`, caught by the gate rather than by care. Gate green
- **[434](434-the-fifth-wake-nobody-counted.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart — **RPC-09 re-swept, and its wake-path count is FIVE.** The lens warns in its own text that the count "must be recounted from the code" because "a sweep that reports the previous sweep's number carries an undercount forward" — 244 said three, 322 corrected to four, and the truth is five: `handleInbound`'s CONNECTION-level grant at `:537` is a separate wake from `_onGrant`'s per-STREAM one at `:378`, has existed since connection flow control did, and **three sweeps walked past it**. The warning was right and still not sufficient — recounting catches an undercount only if it is EXHAUSTIVE, which means enumerating every call site mechanically rather than looking where the last round looked. **The mechanism also MOVED**: a grep for the lens's own names (`_fcAwaitCredit`, `_fcOnGrant`, `_fcForget`) now returns http2 only, because core's parking left `channel_transport.dart` for a dedicated `RpcFlowController` — a detector written against the old names reads as a clean sweep of a file that no longer holds the mechanism. P-10 reused, all eight cells hold, and **its ablation still starving the sender was not a given** once a second wake path was known: refusing only `_onGrant` could have left the connection path waking it, which would have made the bench `broken`. It hangs at 20 s — the sender parks on per-stream credit
- **[433](433-the-count-that-did-not-move.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart and rpc_dart_isolate — **RPC-14 re-swept after 110 rounds and 57 changed files**, and the lens had left instructions for exactly this round: *"Round 323's count, for the next sweep to compare against: 7 in `rpc_dart/lib` ... and 4 in `rpc_dart_isolate`."* Both counts hold, same eleven sites, same disposition — 2 that hold something deliberately, 5 that wait on data, 4 isolate teardowns — with every line number moved, two of them by this same session (`client_connection.dart:514 -> :609` in 430, `isolate_transport_web.dart:316 -> :124` in 428). **The site worth re-reading rather than re-counting was the one round 430 touched**: its `TypeError` catch sits inside the timeout region this lens is about, and `_discardAbandonedAttempt` ends in `.catchError((Object _) {})`, so the downcast 430 made possible is caught rather than reaching the root zone. Control repeated from 323 because the code moved: skipping `teardownStartup()` fails the startup witness at 20 s with the child **still running**. **A lens that records its COUNT costs one grep to re-sweep** — that instruction, written in 323, is what made this the cheapest round available. Ten of eleven sites remain unwitnessed, unchanged and still stated
- **[432](432-the-half-a-user-reads.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart + http2 + isolate — **B-30's `example/` half, and the lead was counting the wrong unit.** 47 in-scope files is **1817 Cyrillic lines** — four times what a file count suggests — and this record demands translation rather than deletion, which is per line. So the scope was stated before starting: the **10 example files, 529 lines**, complete and self-contained, and the half a user actually reads since `example/` ships to pub.dev. Now zero. Translated with the constraints intact (*"a fresh Stream each time, or the second listen throws already listened to"*, *"codecs must not be passed in zero-copy mode"*, *"required: call the parent dispose()"*), and emoji removed in the same pass — including from the text these programs PRINT, which is output rather than source. **1288 lines across 37 `test/` files remain**, and several are test NAMES, which changes what the suite reports and is a different kind of edit. **B-70 was checked first and has nothing to take**: its index line still read *"34 and 23 are the two worth taking next"*, stale by thirteen rounds and refuted by the section directly above them in its own file — corrected here. Gate green
- **[431](431-the-guard-that-guards-nobody.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — **B-39's documentation half shipped; its GUARD half refuted and returned to the owner.** The lead's probe re-runs unchanged 73 rounds on — the root-zone crash is live and the construction-zone fix demonstrably works — but neither the lead nor either of its two decisions asked the third question: **can the crashing arm be reached with a socket the LIBRARY built?** Read at both construction sites, no. `openWebSocket` builds the raw WebSocket locally and never hands it out; the server path says so in its own comment, *"once inside IOWebSocketChannel the socket is no longer reachable"*. The only closer is the library, and every teardown goes through `close()`, which sets `_closed` first — the control row that returns. **So the guard and the defect are disjoint**: the crash needs a raw socket the caller holds, and a caller only holds one when it built the socket itself, in its own zone, which the decided guard explicitly cannot reach. Shipping it would reroute every async error from every library-built channel — the accepted trade — to catch a throw that cannot occur there. What shipped is the residual on `RpcWebSocketChannel`'s class doc, where the one exposed caller reads it: the measured matrix, that neither `isClosed` nor `closeCode` can see it, that a `try`/`catch` does not help, and the two ways to be safe. **Second time these two leads came apart on reachability** — B-35 was left alone in 426 for the same reason. Gate green
- **[430](430-the-guard-the-type-walked-past.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart + 5 transports — **B-21, a decided breaking change, and ONE of its two claims was false.** *"Fixes nothing currently broken"* HOLDS — P-09 reused, control repeated first, reads exactly what round 224 left. *"Round 224's runtime refusal stays either way"* does NOT: with the typed factory a cursorless transport can only arrive through a cast, and **the implicit downcast throws BEFORE the `is!` guard sees it**, degrading a one-shot refusal (`Disconnected`, built once, the two members named) into a backoff spin (`Connecting`, built twice and counting, a bare TypeError) — the exact thing 224's GUARD test exists to prevent. Owner's call on the measurement: **ship the type AND restore the refusal**, so the connect loop now treats a factory `TypeError` as non-transient. The analyzer enumerated the surface — **39 sites, exactly one in library code** once the four caller transports named the marker — and item 2's "five packages, mechanical" was the smaller half: **three entry points had ERASED the capability in their declared type** (`RpcInMemoryTransport.pair`, `RpcIsolateTransport.spawn` x3, `RpcWasmTransport.fromBridge`) while returning objects that conform, so a factory using any of them would not have compiled on working code. Canary is the round's own first attempt: without the TypeError catch, both of 224's guarantees fail and the other seven tests in that file stay green. P-09's readings unchanged after — which is the point, the compile error is new and the runtime behaviour is not. Gate green incl. `test:web`
- **[429](429-two-of-three-were-already-done.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart and rpc_dart_http — **B-09's three gRPC-compat items, and TWO were already done.** The lead's own item 3 states the rule it broke: *"when deferring for blast radius, verify the specific thing you claim would break, or the deferral is a guess wearing a reason's clothes"* — applied to round 89's revert, not to itself. **(2) was fixed before the decision was written**: `grpcStatusFromHttpStatus` is one table for every transport, `429 || 502 || 503 || 504 => unavailable`, with a doc comment making B-09's retryability argument almost word for word. **(3) does not reproduce**: P-97 new, `items=2, error=RpcStatusException` where memory expected `items=2, NO ERROR`, and the third arm is a control proving the probe can report `NO ERROR` at all — without it three arms saying "error" would be equally consistent with an instrument that knows one answer. **(1) was live**, and both halves were the same mistake about one character: gRPC names `,`, RFC 9110 only RECOMMENDS comma-SP. `statusDetailsBin` never split, so a comma-joined value failed `base64.normalize` and read **null** — the error's structured details vanished while its status and message arrived intact — and the HTTP/1.1 caller split on `', '` alone. Fixed both, and rewrote the comment calling this a KNOWN LIMITATION with symmetric loss, which the spec does not agree exists: splitting IS the recombination Custom-Metadata's definition provides for. Canaries narrow each delimiter back and fail only its own transport's witnesses; round 285's unpadded suite stays green, which is what says the `-bin` change did not disturb the neighbouring clause on the same line. Gate green incl. `test:web`
- **[428](428-the-file-the-test-could-not-import.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_isolate — **B-31, and its decided route could not have worked.** Route 3 was sold on "the test runs on the VM, because nothing in the channel's failure path needs `dart:js_interop`" — true of the PATH, and **a test imports a FILE**: `isolate_transport_web.dart` opens with `dart:js_interop` and `package:web`, and a VM test importing it dies in ~20 000 characters of `Type 'JSArrayBuffer' not found`. `@visibleForTesting` makes a class nameable, not a library loadable. **Instead the platform-neutral half moved**: `_WebMultiplexedChannel`, the wire format and the four helpers use `dart:async` and rpc_dart and nothing else, so they now live in `lib/src/web_bridge.dart` and the Worker plumbing stays behind. **Cheaper on this lead's own axis** — route 3 would have annotated a class inside a file the barrel conditionally EXPORTS, while `web_bridge.dart` is exported by nothing, so the public API is unchanged. Round 310's fix finally has a witness, 118 rounds on: a stub `send` and no Worker, asserting both halves the owner demanded plus a third (the channel still RECEIVES). **Two ablations separate the clauses**: `await close(); return;` fails all three witnesses, a bare `return;` fails only the named-error one, and both GUARDs stay green throughout. Gate green incl. `test:web`, which is what compiles the web file for dart2js and runs the package on Chrome
- **[427](427-the-number-the-decision-asked-me-to-write-down.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_compression — **B-29, whose decision names the number to write down: 15980 ms against the VM's 12 ms.** L-13 says the carrying round re-measures the sentence, and that is the whole finding: P-34 reused unchanged reads **13463 ms median of three (13606/13094/13463) against 13 ms** — the defect intact across 141 rounds, the figure moved 16%. Writing it verbatim would have planted what rule one warns about, prose carrying a number that reads like evidence and ages with nothing to check it. So the residual goes into `RpcGzipCodec.maxDecompressedSize`'s doc comment and a new README section (the README had not mentioned the limit existed at all) **as a shape — enforced after allocation, not configurable, "a bound on what you accept, not a defence against what it costs to refuse"** — pointing at the audit test for figures. **Canary: `boundedInflate` disabled reads RSS +2072.1 MiB against a 400 MiB bound**, ~18 s, both GUARDs green. **The FIRST canary was invalid and is recorded because it looked valid**: the same ablation against P-34's forged-trailer fixture reads 22 ms, because `dart:io`'s gzip filter rejects a forged trailer before any inflating — a guard one layer above the one under test, and a wrong causal paragraph was already drafted from it for PUBLIC documentation. The two fixtures are not interchangeable. Gate green; `test:web` not swept (doc-comment-only `lib/` change) and the package's node suite run 3x instead
- **[426](426-the-mirror-nobody-held-up.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-56's PUMP half, the remainder 425 stated.** Of the four pumps two are SINK pumps and genuine twins (`requestSink`, `responseSink`); the other two differ in CONTROL FLOW, not hooks, and are declined on the lead's own "not a base class" constraint. **The twins disagreed on the clause round 390 fixed on one of them**, and B-56's two tables describe it two different ways — the nine-site sweep marks `responseSink` "stop on done: y", the five-site table "n/a, it IS the producer". Neither is measured and they cannot both be right. P-96 new, a handler producing at one message per 5 ms with a consumer reading everything, delta over the 250 ms after the ending: **handler half-closes +32, handler fails the call +35, `responder.done` TRUE for both** — the responder ended the call itself and kept draining its producer — against a +1 control (`responder.close()`) and a +1 caller-side control. **Silent, where 390's was an endless error log**: a send on a finished processor RETURNS rather than throwing. `BidirectionalStreamResponder.done` has existed all along; nothing watched it. Fixed BY extracting `SinkPump`, which owns pause-per-send, half-close, tell-the-peer-once, stop-on-`ended` and a teardown that cancels before closing; each site chooses only its signal. `-207/+69` across two files plus a 121-line helper. **One ablation, both sites red, twice over** — and the stop-clause ablation also reddens round 390's own witness, written 36 rounds ago for the other class. Arms B and C (the peer leaving) are unchanged and stated: `responder.done` reads FALSE there, so there is no signal to watch. Gate green incl. `test:web`
- **[425](425-the-cancel-path-the-table-had-no-column-for.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-56's extraction, carried out for the BRIDGE half, and the scope taken before the fix rather than after it.** Reading the nine sites apart gives two mechanics its one table conflates: six BRIDGES (mirror a source through a controller we own) and four PUMPS (pause the subscription per send) — and the sweep's nine miss `_pumpBidirectionalResponses` entirely. **A bridge has TWO cancel paths and the table has one column**: round 424 fixed the owner-teardown half, and two of the six still RETURNED the source's cancel Future from `onCancel`, which `StreamController` awaits. P-95 new, consumer `cancel()` over a parked user `async*`, 3000 ms cap: `track` **HUNG**, the circuit breaker **HUNG**, against a 6 ms control with the await removed — and no `disposerTimeout` bounds this path, unlike the one 424 fixed. **Arm 5 is L-13's premise check and it split the decision in two**: site 6, the designated extraction source, reads 7 ms on a real server-stream call parked in `await for (responses)` — sound — while the sentence about the other copies was not. Fixed BY extracting: `StreamBridge` owns pause forwarding, close-on-done and both cancel paths; all six bridges go through it, `-158/+62` across 8 files plus a 119-line helper, two ad-hoc guards subsumed. **ONE ablation of its `onCancel` turns both witnesses red** — which is the property the extraction buys and the only claim a per-file fix could not have made. The four pumps are a different signature and are stated as not done. Gate green incl. `test:web`
- **[424](424-the-rule-written-thirty-lines-above.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-56's three DEFECTS, not its refactor.** `RpcCallScope.listen` ended with `onDispose(sub.cancel)`, and disposers are AWAITED with `disposerTimeout` as the only bound — so closing a scope holding a parked generator cost **five seconds per subscription**, measured by the canary at the full budget against a 2000 ms witness bound. **Thirty lines above it, `track` does the same job correctly and states both rules and their price** — *"Not awaited: cancelling a suspended generator can block indefinitely ... a rejected cancel became TWO unhandled async errors, measured, and enough to kill the isolate"* — and `listen` was written with neither; the circuit breaker's bare `sub.cancel()` on a detached timer is the second half of that same sentence in another file. Also: neither `track` nor `listen` forwarded pause/resume, so a slow consumer never slowed the SOURCE. **The lesson is not the difficulty but where the rules already were: in a COMMENT, not in a function** — which is exactly what B-56's extraction is for. That extraction is NOT done and this round does not claim it; it needs one call site at a time with its witness green after each, and it has a designated source (site 6, the only one carrying the "fire the token only if the stream did not already finish" clause). Gate green incl. `test:web`
- **[423](423-the-third-route.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **B-57, and the third route the owner asked for a round to look for.** Both known routes were closed, and they failed for the SAME reason: each asked WHERE the handler should subscribe. It should not — the duty the subscription carried is not the handler's, it is the connection's, and the connection already has exactly one subscription. **P-94 new, a COUNT rather than a duration** (a wall clock on an in-memory pair measures the machine): listeners on `incomingMessages` per parked unary handler go `2/11/51/201` at 1/10/50/200 to a flat **1**, the control being one flag. For a pipeline responder the subscription delivered NOTHING anyway — the pipeline builds it with `id: streamId` and hands it the request four lines later. What it carried was `onError`, and `responder_pipeline`'s own onError **logged only**, which is exactly why round 393 refused to drop it; `_answerActiveStreams` now answers every stream once through `wireStatusFor` instead of N listeners answering one each. `onDone` stays separate: there the transport is gone and there is nothing to answer over. **The GUARD passes on BOTH sides of the ablation**, which is what says the duty moved rather than being dropped. **The reading the lead asked for was never taken** — whether a non-advisory error can arrive without the channel closing — and the fix made it moot rather than settling it; stated as declined. Gate green incl. `test:web`
- **[422](422-the-number-that-decided-a-shutdown.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart + http2 + websocket — **B-63's four remaining mechanics**, ranked before starting because they are not equal. **`_inFlightCalls` was the one with teeth**: both servers read the drain's count out of `collectEndpointMetrics()['activeResponders']` with `as int? ?? 0` — an OBSERVABILITY key, free to rename — so renaming it made both count ZERO, every drain complete instantly, and the shutdown report success while cutting live calls off. `drainUntilIdle` was already shared and the number feeding it was not, which is the half deciding whether a shutdown waits at all; and **rounds 418 and 421 had just made it heavier**, since `RpcApp` now delegates shutdown to that drain. Now `RpcEndpointBase.activeResponderCount`, typed, with the metrics key reading FROM it. **The canary's reading is the whole finding**: renaming the key fails the test that watches the coupling and LEAVES THE WEBSOCKET DRAIN PASSING — before, that rename broke the drain silently with every test green. `_notify` → `notifyWithoutDying`, four responder sites → `sendWireError`. **`_startKeepalive` is half-extracted on purpose** — the loop, the latch and the `isDead` guard the server half lacked are shared; the failure RESPONSE is not, because the two differ deliberately, and forcing them together is the cosmetic unification this lens declines. Filed **B-71** while here: a browser WebSocket client has no half-open detection at all, because the browser owns ping/pong and `pingInterval` is accepted and dropped. Gate green incl. `test:web`
- **[421](421-a-stopped-server-that-answers.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http2 + rpc_dart_websocket — **B-58 and B-59**, both owner-decided, both about what a server owes a peer it will not serve. **B-58's discriminator did not exist until round 412**: the site read `error is RpcException`, the BASE of the hierarchy, so a resource limit and malformed framing matched the same branch — 412 gave them separate statuses (8 and 13), which is what finally makes "count the protocol violations and not the limits" expressible. RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED stays excluded: that peer is misconfigured, not broken, and is told so precisely to correct itself. **B-59's mode flag alone was WRONG TWICE and the existing suite found both** — `start()` listened AGAIN, adding a second listener to a broadcast source so every connection got two endpoints and the call failed; and close code 1001 "going away" is the right meaning and an unsendable mechanism, since `package:web_socket` reserves everything outside 1000 and 3000-4999. Neither was in the decision and neither would have been found by reading. **Four existing tests inverted, and B-59's baseline carried its own inversion instruction** — *"if a stopped server now closes what it cannot serve, B-59 is fixed and this expectation is the thing to invert"*. `stop()` keeps its subscription and refuses arrivals; `dispose()` is the new terminal step, so a restart over a single-subscription stream now works. Gate green incl. `test:web`
- **[420](420-three-decisions-that-invented-a-result.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart + rpc_blob — **B-40, B-36 and B-46**, three owner decisions written down and never carried out, sharing a shape worth naming: in each the code SUBSTITUTED a result for one it did not have — a dotted service name became `/UnknownService/UnknownMethod`, an unobserved probe was recorded as a SUCCESS that closed the breaker, an empty blob id became a generated one. **B-40's edit was small and in the wrong place.** The first witness reimplemented the rule and checked itself — it would have passed with the production code reverted — because the formatter was private on a mixin RPC-24 hides. The fix was to MOVE it beside its inverse in `metadata.dart`, and the test now asserts the property against both real functions: the formatter rebuilds everything the parser admits. **An inverse pair that cannot be read together is an inverse pair that drifts.** B-36's pinned test moved exactly as its record predicted — `closed` was also what a fabricated success produced, so it could not tell the two apart; it now asserts `halfOpen` PLUS a following call that is admitted. B-46 got both implementations of `IBlobClient`, and its adjacent check came back NEGATIVE and is recorded: the adapters' empty-id substitution IS the deliberate wire encoding. Three canaries; B-46's left both CONTROLs green, so it is not "refuse everything without an id". Gate green incl. `test:web`
- **[419](419-asking-is-not-the-same-as-destroying.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http2 + rpc_dart_isolate — **B-70's two behavioural items**, 23 and 34. **23: the only way to learn the answer was to make it true.** Whether a `viaSocket` transport can rebuild its connection is fixed at CONSTRUCTION — it said so by passing `() => throw ...('does not support reconnect')`, and `reconnect()` calls the factory LAST, after discarding the connection, cancelling every subscription, disposing every pump and clearing six per-stream maps. Every in-flight call died to produce an error known before the method was entered. The factory is nullable now and null is checked BEFORE the teardown. **34: a `Worker` has no argument list.** The VM ships the policy as `args[4]` and the worker rebuilds it; on web `spawn(policy:)` applied it to the HOST transport only while the worker-side function took its own default — so a raised limit held on one side of the boundary and not the other, silently. It rides on the worker URL now, the one channel a Worker has at construction. **An existing suite used the defect as a TOOL**: `reconnect_failure_is_recoverable_test`'s helper is documented as "a transport whose reconnect factory ALWAYS throws, by construction" and used `viaSocket` precisely because it did — so the answer is a SPLIT, not a replacement: `viaSocket` takes an `@visibleForTesting connectionFactory`, and "cannot reconnect" (refuse, destroy nothing) and "the attempt failed" (tear down, stay recoverable) are covered separately.
