One shape, RPC-04: a capability present in the implementation and unreachable through the type the caller holds. **B-68 is an ORDERING defect and the unreachable parameter is why** — all three servers implemented `stop({drainTimeout})`, `IRpcServer` declared `stop()`, so `RpcApp` compensated by draining the endpoints itself: nothing had stopped the LISTENER, so a connection arriving mid-window got an already-draining endpoint, and `RpcEndpointBase.drain` CANCELS active contexts, the opposite of letting in-flight work finish — the very operation the websocket server's comment warns against since round 414. Now the server drains and goes FIRST, so a handler can still reach module resources while it finishes. **B-69 is a defect in the WITNESS**: `pair()` gave both halves the server's oversized-frame policy, so the frame-codec harness handed every suite a "client" that kills its connection. **The suite was green before the fix and after** — which is the finding, not a gap: the flag's two behaviours are unreachable from a `pair()` client today, so nothing was asserting the wrong half *yet*. **The interface cost, measured rather than estimated: six test fakes in one package.** Both canaries clean; all CONTROLs and GUARDs green. Now one table in `protocol.dart` and one grammar in `metadata.dart` with the limit passed in. **The hypothesis "adopt the better-argued copy and delete the other" broke on an EXISTING test**: the http2 table is grpc-go's `HTTPStatusConvTab` exactly, but `oversized_request_is_resource_exhausted_test` named its direction — rpc_dart's own responders answer 413 for a body over `maxMessageLengthBytes`, and RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED is what tells the caller it hit a SIZE it can reduce. 413 and 499 are kept beyond grpc-go's rows and the reason is written down; 409/410/412/415/501 and the `>=400` default are gone. **The first canary failed the witness AND its guard** — with the number hardcoded the knob is ignored in both directions, which is a different defect from the one the witness alone describes. - **[416](416-every-error-names-its-status.md)** FIXED, 17 packages — **the owner's call, twice mid-round: "get rid of all StateError" and "backward compatibility does not matter"**, which overturns half of round 411's hand-made classification and is recorded as a RETRACTION rather than applied quietly. **80 `throw StateError` sites in `lib/` across 17 packages, plus 9 that build one as a value** — the second group missed by the first grep and caught by the owner naming `buffered_broadcast.dart:213`. The damage was already written in the code's own words at `context.dart:461`: `wireStatusFor` is default-deny, so every one of these inside a handler reached the peer as INTERNAL "Internal server error" with its reason destroyed — a chunk-size limit the caller could have corrected, a checksum mismatch (corruption, indistinguishable from a server bug), every optimistic-concurrency conflict. Now each names a status the caller can act on: RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED for limits, ABORTED for version conflicts, DATA_LOSS for checksums and dropped events, INVALID_ARGUMENT for upload framing and decoded peer payloads, UNIMPLEMENTED, UNAVAILABLE, FAILED_PRECONDITION. **`RpcClosedException` is a TYPE because the MESSAGE was load-bearing and could not be**: `_isTransportClosed` compared message TEXT in two spellings, because the transports disagreed on what to throw — and `channel_transport.dart:437` had already drifted, so the matcher was widened to cope instead of the drift removed. That is B-63's largest item, live. **B-64 closed out in the same pass**: `RpcCallerTrailer` owns the trailer rule for all five call shapes, `RpcLongTimer.timeout` replaces the four bare `Future.timeout` deadline bounds, and the pre-flight guard covers all five. **Six canaries and three of them changed the round** — one passed and exposed a test that could not tell the endpoint guard from the transport's (re-pointed at `what == 'Endpoint'`), one passed and exposed a claim not witnessable at all (`then<void>`; kept, no canary claimed), and a GUARD caught a real unhandled-error bug in the NEW helper before it shipped. Also: `closeResponderResources` was the one teardown of five that never told the handler; `UnaryResponder` answered DATA + `grpc-status: 0` on a stream the drain had already closed out, on both branches, while every streaming shape was gated on `_isActive`; the http2 RESPONDER did a bare `await finish()` where its caller sibling bounds it and terminates; the CORS lists named 3 headers against the 7 core sends. **Two of the five were not the edit the sweep described, both in the copy that had drifted furthest** — `ping.dart` does not call the shared factory at all, so changing its literal would have made the message worse with nothing gained; and the token cancel made a LATENT `RpcCallScope.close()` race reachable for the first time, caught by an existing test (`['third','hang-start']` against `['third','hang-start','first']`) that no new test would have looked for. **Seven canaries, one per half**; two of them changed the round — the first caller-site ablation PASSED, exposing `server/caller.dart:199` as changed-but-unwitnessed, and one CORS test passed on both sides and was rewritten to assert what the fix actually claims. - **[413](413-the-envelope-that-threw-it-away.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http2 — B-62, which 409 deferred, and **the deferral did not survive round 412**. Fixed by `extends RpcStatusException` deriving status and message **THROUGH `wireStatusFor(error)`** rather than copying the inner's fields: that is the whole safety argument, since `wireStatusFor` is the single place deciding what may leave the process, so a FOREIGN inner is still redacted where copying `inner.statusCode` would have silently opened the deny for everyone else's errors. `streamId` and `error` untouched, routing unchanged. Left: `filterStreamEvents` still has no call site, but that now costs precision rather than correctness

Measured one prefix apart: `limit grpc-status 8` and `malformed grpc-status 8`, where RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED is retryable — a corrupt frame answered "try again". Now `8` and `13`. **The owner's question caught a mistake of mine**: I had made the decompression site RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED, and the code's own comment had predicted it — *"a decompressor throws for the bomb it was asked to stop AND for input that is malformed ... naming one of them told a peer with a corrupt frame to send less, which cannot help"*. Corrected to INTERNAL, which is also grpc-go's answer. Switching that site to `wireStatusFor` **also closed a leak**: its trailer message was `'$error'` UNCONDITIONALLY, so a foreign error's text went to the peer past default-deny. **`RpcException` is now abstract**, which is what forced the remaining 13 raw sites to choose — UNIMPLEMENTED for unsupported grpc-encoding ×2 (the gRPC spec's own answer) and an unregistered method, RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED for three exhaustion limits, INTERNAL for the rest — and surfaced 31 test sites, the visible half of the breaking cost. `e is RpcException` still means "ours", asserted. What does not belong is `ArgumentError` for **untrusted peer input**, and `validateMetadata` threw exactly that, with **five decision points reading `is ArgumentError` to mean "the peer is at fault"** — two of them deciding whether to END A CONNECTION via the 256-violation backstop. Fixed with `RpcMetadataViolation extends RpcStatusException implements ArgumentError` — **implementing `ArgumentError` is the load-bearing half**, since all five sites and any caller's `catch` keep working while the type joins the hierarchy and carries INVALID_ARGUMENT instead of a redacted INTERNAL; then the two backstops narrow to `is RpcMetadataViolation`, which is the point. **The first fix was incomplete and two tests said so** — `"a grinding peer loses the connection at the default policy"` and its server mirror went red, because `http2HeadersToRpcMetadata` enforces `maxHeaders` DURING the walk rather than through `validateMetadata`, so its own throw was what the backstop actually counted. L-12 live: the class had a member I had not counted. `_headerValue`'s check deliberately stays an ordinary `ArgumentError` — its four callers all build OUTBOUND headers from our own metadata. Canary asserts the violation is all three types AT ONCE (dropping the `ArgumentError` interface would silently disarm two connection-closing backstops) and that a foreign `ArgumentError` is neither. Fixed with a named constructor per kind, default staying INTERNAL so the six malformed sites and `platform_error_redaction_test` are unchanged. `wireStatusFor` untouched: same function, different branch, because the type moved. Canary adds the default-is-still-INTERNAL assertion (the regression that would silently re-flatten six sites) and that the diagnostic still forwards. **Gate reported honestly: 2 of 8 workspace runs went red, neither reproducible nor nameable** — one from a chained shell line, one `rpc_dart_websocket +176 -1` with no test named — against six greens, four back-to-back, and websocket 3/3 alone. L-14's tell says a real defect survives the remedy a flake responds to and this did not; L-14 also says NAME it, and I could not, so it is recorded as unnamed rather than dismissed, because the change touches `channel_transport.dart` which websocket uses
- **[409](409-the-transports-two-types.md)** DEFERRED, rpc_dart_http2 and rpc_dart_websocket — the owner's goal names the transports and 408 stopped at core. **Five transports declare TWO error types between them**: `RpcWebSocketNonBinaryFrame extends RpcException implements IRpcAdvisoryChannelError`, and `RpcHttp2StreamError`, which is not an Exception at all. RPC-24. The hypothesis — that the second is 408's defect again — is **half right, and the other half is the finding**: it is not an error but an ENVELOPE, so "not an Exception" is correct by design, because nobody is meant to catch it before `filterStreamEvents` unwraps it. **Except nothing unwraps it.** Every reference in the repository is its own declaration, its own doc comment, a CHANGELOG line, and five uses in one test against a hand-made `StreamController` — **zero production call sites against two that CREATE the envelope**. So `wireStatusFor`, which is default-deny (408's subject), sees a non-`RpcException` and takes the deny branch: whatever the inner error was, the peer is told INTERNAL "Internal server error". The envelope hides its contents from the one function whose job is to decide what may be forwarded. Per-stream routing does not depend on it — both transports already `_streams.addError(streamId, error, stackTrace)` with the RAW inner error beside the enveloped broadcast add — so the envelope serves the broadcast alone and the broadcast has no unwrapper. **DEFERRED because the two fixes are opposite** and choosing needs a number neither has: restore the design by wiring `filterStreamEvents` in, or drop the envelope so the broadcast carries a classifiable error — the latter tempting until you notice connection-level fatal errors are added UNENVELOPED on purpose, which is the distinction the envelope exists to draw. **B-62** names the measurement that decides it. A sweep naming every reference is evidence about the CODE, not about the cost, and ending there rather than guessing is the point. No gate — no code changed
- **[408](408-three-types-outside-the-hierarchy.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — the owner's new goal, one typed error hierarchy; L-12 first, so the CLASS is counted before anything moves. **199 throw sites** across core, framework and five transports — `45 ArgumentError / 38 StateError / 31 FormatException / 22 RpcException / 17 RpcStatusException / 15 UnsupportedError / 11 TimeoutException / 8 RpcFrameException / …` — **131 of 199 raw Dart errors, and a mass rename would be wrong**: `ArgumentError` for a real programming mistake is idiomatic, and 25 of the FormatExceptions are in the CBOR codec where malformed input is exactly that type's job. The class that matters is narrower: library DOMAIN concepts outside the library's hierarchy, and there are three — `RpcCancelledException`, `RpcDeadlineExceededException`, `CircuitBreakerOpenException`, all `implements Exception`. **The hypothesis that this costs nothing came from `wireStatusFor`'s own doc** (*"the responder pipeline answers both with their own status long before an error is translated"*) — round 321's variant of RPC-15, a comment explaining a previous fix. **Refuted**: `wireStatusFor` is DEFAULT DENY, so `cancelled` and `deadline` both came back `status 13 "Internal server error"` — **indistinguishable from a foreign StateError**, where gRPC has CANCELLED(1) and DEADLINE_EXCEEDED(4) for exactly this. The three controls are what made it readable (`rpcException` keeps its message, `statusException` keeps status and message, `stateError` redacted). **The cited cycle did not block the fix**: it blocks `protocol.dart` importing `context.dart` — the MAPPER importing the types — not the types extending the base, and `contracts/_index.dart` already imports `RpcException` in its own `show` list. So the fix is at the TYPE and `wireStatusFor` was not touched, which is why the deny stayed exactly as strict: `1 "handler cancelled"` and `4 "Deadline … exceeded"`, every control unchanged. `RpcException`/`RpcStatusException` became `const` (additive) because two subclasses were already const and `const CircuitBreakerOpenException()` appears twice in its own file; the breaker keeps `retryAfter` in `toString()` for the same reason, so its text is byte-identical. Seven-test canary of which **four are GUARDs**, because widening a hierarchy is exactly the change that can widen a security boundary. Gate 14/14, rpc_dart **+1573**, and **no existing test needed changing**. Named, not touched: `RpcHttp2StreamError` is not an Exception, `RpcFrameException` extends the wrong base, and the 83 raw StateError/ArgumentError sites need a reachability split first

A call in flight when the worker dies is ANSWERED rather than left hanging. `isClosed: true` is not a fourth disagreement: a killed isolate is terminal, there is nothing to reconnect to, and the socket transports stay `false` because they can recover — same column, different correct answer, because the state model genuinely differs. The three-transport table now reads `websocket 9 / http2 14 / isolate 14`, and **this round deliberately draws no conclusion from it**: which status wins is an unanswered question put to the owner, so the row goes into the lead and not into an argument. No gate — no library code changed
- **[406](406-one-refusal-shape.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket and rpc_dart_http2 — B-61, with the owner answering both of 405's questions: unify the type on `RpcStatusException(FAILED_PRECONDITION)`, and make the http2 caller enter the disconnected state when its connection dies. **The first shipped; the second could not, and that is the round's finding.** Both `_ensureUsable` implementations now throw `RpcStatusException(failedPrecondition)`, so one `catch` covers every transport — and the CODE is the load-bearing part, not the type: `RpcRetryInterceptor._shouldRetry` takes only UNAVAILABLE and RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED and never calls `reconnect()`, so FAILED_PRECONDITION keeps the earlier round's argument (a retryable refusal invites the caller to repeat what cannot work) under a catchable type. **The second half was implemented, then reverted on evidence**: a derived `!isOpen && !goawayReceived && no active streams` predicate broke two tests that each pin a deliberate opposite — *"a drained connection is retried as the retry doc promises"* and *"GUARD: a genuinely dead connection still reports UNAVAILABLE and down"*, the latter being exactly B-61's named ending and requiring `createStream()` to hand out an id. **So B-61's premise was wrong**: http2 does not fail to notice its connection died, it notices and answers UNAVAILABLE-and-retryable on purpose, while websocket refuses non-retryably on purpose. Two rounds, two defensible choices, each pinned with its argument. A first attempt was worse and is recorded: it dropped the GOAWAY exemption because health's own message says *"Reconnect is required; 0 call(s) still finishing"* — **a predicate lifted out of a reporting function inherits the report's distinctions, not the caller's**, and health splits draining from down to say different things while the send path splits them to decide whether a retry can work. Refuted by the probe's design: every arm polls the transport's own `health()` until it stops reporting healthy, and prints it, before touching the guard. `websocket degraded / createStream() StateError / a unary call StateError` against `http2 degraded / createStream() **no throw** / a unary call RpcStatusException`, `isClosed false` on both. **Both reached the state; only one guard fires**, and `createStream()` is the decisive row because it calls `_ensureUsable` directly on both — on http2 it hands out an id on a dead connection. **Mechanism to the line**: `_disconnected` has exactly two writers on the http2 caller, the KEEPALIVE failure path and the catch inside `reconnect()`, and `pingInterval` is opt-in — so the ordinary ending, the server goes away and nobody was pinging, reaches neither and the flag stays false forever. P-89 drives the three highest-risk — `RpcApp`'s *"create a new RpcApp to restart"*, the closest match to 401's shape, and both copies of *"call reconnect()"* — and **all three are correct**: `RpcApp` restarts on every arm including the literal reading where only the App is new and the module instances are reused (configured, started and stopped a second time), and both halves of the two-claim reconnect sentence hold on both transports. **The round's product is a sharpened detector**: the tell is not "a message that prescribes", it is *a message that prescribes rebuilding A when the state that blocks you is held by B* — `RpcApp.server` takes a server BUILDER, so the closure rebuilds what cannot be re-listened, where the websocket server said rebuild the server while the obstacle was the stream. Both guards read correct and the connection still dies, so what is left sits between them, inside `package:http2`, invisible from either end's logs. **Why the obvious fix is a TRADE and stays the owner's**: suppressing the reset for a stream we have already half-closed fixes this and breaks what cancellation is for — a client that half-closes at once and then abandons a long server-stream download needs that reset — and both are the same wire state, distinguishable only by whether the peer has finished, which is the fact rpc_dart learns too late. Two routes that are not trades: ask package:http2 for the stream state (its API does not expose it — a small upstream request), or report it upstream, where the reproduction is now four arms and one ablated statement

- **[366](366-the-end-that-sailed-past-a-waiting-message.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — **brought in from outside the journal**: a consumer's blob uploads began failing on the release that bumped 5.0.1 → 6.0.0 and on nothing else, the server refusing the same blob fifteen times with `Declared length 2442197 does not match received 524288 bytes` and not one reconnect in the logs. RPC-01, whose shape is credit accounting whose release is decided elsewhere. An end-of-stream carries no payload, so nothing meters it — and `finishSending` wrote it immediately, **overtaking a `sendMessage` still parked in `_fcAwaitCredit`**. The peer then sees a stream that ended with N-1 messages and a clean end, indistinguishable from a sender with nothing more to say, while the sender is told nothing because the parked send is never woken. Witness on pristine HEAD: `Expected: RpcStatusException with statusCode 14 / Actual: 'never completed'`. Fixed by marking the stream finished and WAITING for a parked send before the end goes out; only parked sends are tracked, because an unconditional await adds a microtask hop to every send and that hop has reordered frames on this transport before. **Two canaries and only one carried weight** — removing the wait reproduced the truncation (`Expected: <4> Actual: <3>`), while a second refusal in `_fcAwaitCredit` left both tests green and was dropped rather than committed, *a change no canary can kill is a change with no evidence behind it*. Bench **P-58**, which exists because `RpcChannelTransport.pair()` answers "never parks" in every row: the parked state needs a real socket and a real RTT, and park duration tracks RTT exactly (20/40/200 ms). Trap kept: **the credit gate admits on `credit > 0`, not on whether the message FITS**, so a 256 KiB frame passes a 64 KiB window and the SECOND frame is the one that parks — reading the constants predicts the opposite, and did. Left open as **B-47**: the initial window is smaller than a message this library's own users send, which is a shipped-default trade the owner must make. **This round did not run `loop.py lint`** — see round 367, which found and repaired its four unindexed records and their off-schema frontmatter
- **[365](365-the-clock-one-sandbox-does-not-have.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_wasm — the owner's item 8 and the iOS half of item 15, both unblocked when a simulator came up mid-session, plus B-42 closed. **The first two-platform device gate of the session** — Android `+24 ~2`, iOS `+26` — and running the SAME guest on both is what turned three open questions into a defect. Sent to ask whether an offscreen WKWebView throttles guest timers: it does not, iOS median lag 0-1 ms up to a 100 ms delay and ~99 ms on a 1 s timer, Android a flat 3-7 ms. **The finding was the other arm**: the identical guest, identical call, threw `RpcStatusException(13): Internal server error` on Android. dart2wasm's glue calls `performance.now()` (`guest.mjs:123`); a WKWebView is a full browser environment and supplies it, `JavaScriptSandbox` is a bare V8 isolate and does not — so **any guest using `Stopwatch` crashed on Android and worked on iOS**, opaquely. Fixed with a `Date.now()` shim installed only when `performance` is absent; millisecond resolution, so a guest timing sub-millisecond intervals reads 0 rather than a wrong number. The canary is also the field symptom — disabling the shim reproduces exactly what a user sees. **Item 8's defensive fix deliberately NOT applied**: the ordering it defends was measured to hold over 10000 frames on both platforms, and chaining the fetches would serialise every send, removing the pipelining those figures rest on — a speed trade, and the config says ASK before making one. Filed as **B-43** with three options and no recommendation. Keepers: **ablating each language separately is not the same as running the same code on both** — 348's rule catches an unported fix, this is a gap in what the two HOST ENVIRONMENTS provide for free, so list the JS globals each boot script assumes; and **a bench built for one question found the defect**, because the timer probe was the first guest code here to use a `Stopwatch`

RPC-23's **strongest and cheapest-to-detect form: prose naming a component that CANNOT do the job.** The README said iOS used `JavaScriptCore`, twice — and JSC has no WebAssembly, so if it were true the package could not run a dart2wasm guest on iOS at all, which it does through an offscreen `WKWebView` with a custom scheme handler. Refutable from the component's own capabilities, no diff archaeology; and a search for the NAME caught both mentions where a read-through would not. Corrected WITH the reason, so it cannot come back. The cost question had no answer in the README at all, so it was measured: **`empty unary p50 12.9 ms`, `1 KiB 12.3 ms`, `64 KiB 15.5 ms`, `1 MiB 114.9 ms`** on an Android 11 emulator — published with the hardware named and the SHAPE explained (flat to 1 KiB because the price is the boundary not the payload; p99 ~10x p50 because a call can miss a driver tick). **The claim written into the websocket README was verified rather than asserted**, and its source was a repository comment — exactly what this lens says not to trust: running the existing probe turned it into `96 MiB sent → 1 chunk → 96 MiB`, and `RpcWebSocketServer` was grepped for a connection limit before the README stated there is none. Documentation has no switch to flip, so that verification IS the canary. The unmeasured iOS timer latency is named in the README where a reader would otherwise assume parity. Written without a regex in either language, so a trimmed `hasPrefix` means the same thing in Kotlin and Swift where no two regex engines would. **Shipped on BOTH platforms with only one witnessed, departing from round 357's revert on stated grounds** — 357 changed a live path's behaviour, this adds a check to a path already failing, so a false negative leaves today's behaviour and the dangerous direction is the one Android measured. RPC-06's Ask is *was this code RUN, or only read and compiled?* — round 357 answered "compiled"; this round RAN it five times and **the code disagreed with the hypothesis**. The code fact is certain: `CoroutineScope(Dispatchers.Main)` with `Base64.decode` over batches of up to 4 MiB per drain, on the thread that dispatches every platform channel in the app. The measurement says it is not the cost: `before 22/59`, `after 12/70`, **`ABLATED 39/38`** (idle worst / busy worst, ms) — with Base64 put back on Main the main thread is no less available than with the fix. Idle worst ranges 12-39 ms, the size of the effect being looked for. **Run 5 is the canary and it is why nothing shipped**: the fix switched off produced no worse a result than the fix in place, so per *no failing witness, no fix* the change was REVERTED. The negative is the product: the occupancy IS real (busy clearly worse than its own control in 3 of 5 runs) but comes from work that cannot be moved — `evaluateJavaScriptAsync` marshalling 4 MiB across Binder, and `messenger.send`, which must be on Main by contract. **The item's criterion is also unmeetable as written**: "no frames >16 ms" on an emulator whose IDLE control alone reaches 39 ms. - **[361](361-the-only-place-to-authenticate.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_websocket — the owner's list, P2 item 13, **and the headers half is not hygiene**: a websocket client can authenticate in exactly ONE place, the upgrade request, because there is no second round trip to attach a token to — so `connect()` could not be used against an authenticating server at all. RPC-22 from the CLIENT's side of the pre-protocol stage rounds 274-275 established for the server. - **[360](360-two-routes-into-one-concept.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — the owner's list, P2 item 14, three core defects filed as one line and taken as one round because they share a shape: each produces a WRONG ANSWER rather than failing, and each has a SIBLING beside it that gets the same question right (RPC-25). **(a)** `RpcStreamIdManager` takes a resume cursor two ways — the `resumeAfter()` METHOD aligns parity and documents it, the `resumeAfter:` CONSTRUCTOR parameter took it raw — so a CLIENT resumed at 4 issued **6, 8, 10**, the SERVER's half of one shared id space, where everything downstream (half-close, release, flow-control credit, `_statusSeen`) is keyed on the id alone. `IRpcStreamIdSequence` states the contract it broke: *"must leave parity intact"*. **(c)** the parser's decompress `catch` rewrapped EVERY throw as *"Decompressed gRPC payload exceeds the configured limit"* — but a decompressor throws for the bomb it was asked to stop AND for malformed or non-compressed input, and the catch cannot tell them apart, so a peer with a CORRUPT frame was told to send less, which cannot help. Both fixed; `_alignedStart` gives the parity rule one home. **(b) DECLINED and filed as B-40**: `_methodPathFromKey` cannot round-trip a dotted service name its sibling `_parseMethodPath` explicitly admits — real drift, and the ordinary path measured CLEAN, so nothing reaches it and the damage is a diagnostic string. Keepers: **two ways to set the same field are two implementations** — point the detector at FIELDS, not only classes — and **drift is not automatically a defect; it is a defect where something reaches it**

RPC-19 back on a FLAG after 353 widened it to signals, and the defect is a state the flag has **no value for**: `_disconnected` is written only from the OUTCOME of a reconnect — `false` on success, `true` in the catch — while `_reconnectOnce` closes `_inner` and THEN awaits the factory for a whole handshake. Nothing describes the duration, so `_ensureUsable` passed over a closed inner for tens to hundreds of ms. Six methods against three states: **the in-window column should equal one of the two controls and equalled neither** — `createStream id=3`, `sendMessage returned` (accepted and dropped by the corpse, so the caller waits out a deadline for a frame never sent), and `getMessagesForStream` answering **`RpcStatusException(14)`, which is RETRYABLE** and invites the caller to repeat what cannot work. One line fixes it, `_disconnected = true` right after `_inner.close()`, and the in-window column becomes the disconnected column. Second half: `sendDirectObject` was the one send method of three without `_ensureUsable` — and it has **no reachable failure**, because `supportsZeroCopy` is false so the caller pipeline refuses a codec-free call first; fixed for consistency and witnessed at the transport level, where the guard changes the answer from the inner's `UnsupportedError` to the explicit `StateError`. Two canaries, each failing only its own witnesses. **The GUARD that earns its place is "the window ENDS"** — a transport that refused forever would pass both witnesses and be far worse than the defect. Keeper: **a flag written from a `catch` describes an outcome, and an outcome is not a state**
- **[358](358-the-catch-that-catches-nothing.md)** DEFERRED, rpc_dart_websocket — the owner's list, P1 item 9, which round 353's fixture had already tripped over. The item proposes the fix (*"send → no-op"*) and **the proposed fix does not work, measured**: `try { _ws.sink.add(data); } catch (_) {}` was applied and re-run and the crash is identical, because `WebSocketSink.add` queues into a StreamController and the real `sendBytes` runs a microtask later in the zone that CONTROLLER was built in — so a `catch` at the call site sees nothing, and neither does a `runZonedGuarded` there. Not unchecked — `_headerValue` rejects non-printable-ASCII, and both transports refuse a Cyrillic value — but **a hardcoded check is not a policy**: under `maxHeaders: 32, maxHeaderValueBytes: 64`, **64 headers, a 200-char value and a header name with a space were all ACCEPTED** where the shared layer threw. Blast radius is ONE stream (clean calls before and after return `ok(x)`), which is what kept it a lead. Ablating the single-flight marker: `Actual: <2>`, `opened=4 closed=2, still live after 30007ms` — the full budget against 1735 ms healthy, which settles it as an orphan rather than slowness; the sequential GUARD stayed green, the right specificity. **Does NOT claim to have fixed CI** — what it fixes is that the next occurrence will say which cause. P-37 asks the controller what arrived rather than asking the guard: of four configurations **two disagreed, and one is a MUTE** — `setTagLevel('verbose', internal)` on a tagged scope delivers the record, the guard says false, so with the guard in place it is never offered. The call still succeeds; nothing reports anything. Reachable through ordinary use: the endpoints build untagged scopes, but **every transport takes a `LogScope?` directly** and turning one tag up is how you get verbose output from one subsystem — that configuration silenced all 41 guarded lines in http2. `child()` carries the tag, so it holds at any depth. Keeper: **a guard is a PREDICTION about other code, only as good as the arguments it makes the prediction with** — and 337's witness could not catch this because it configured the logger the way the guard happened to assume

Replaced with an **edge-triggered read at the peer** — hold the socket, require `408`, which this transport emits at exactly one place after registration while every pre-registration refusal answers its own code. Same computation, different LIFETIME: once per registration there, once per call here. Merging would carry a registration rule onto a call path — dead at best, refusing a second call at worst → **C-34**. Second CLEAN in a row, and the pair shows the lens DECLINING, which is what its "the finding is the drift" bar protects
- **[316](316-a-convincing-argument-that-was-wrong.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart — takes 315's open thread (the zero-copy generic asymmetry) and reads the dispatch. The reading produced a convincing defect: the registry's static type is `<Object, Object>`, Dart function parameters are contravariant, so the `as` in `callUnaryHandler` should throw and zero-copy unary should be broken outright. **It is wrong — Dart generics are REIFIED**, so the method runs with the instance's real type arguments and the cast is an identity. One grep found `unsendable_direct_object_test.dart:120` already exercising the exact shape predicted to fail, green. The asymmetry is cosmetic; no code changed → **B-32**. Fourth time in eight rounds that a conclusion was reached by reading where a grep was cheap
- **[315](315-eight-places-for-one-rule.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart — RPC-25's **first application to core**, on the owner's target: the responder contract's four `add*Method` registrations. `_rejectDuplicate` — "is this method name taken", a check that reads BOTH maps and so cannot depend on the branch — was called from **eight** places, once per branch per method, alongside four byte-identical preambles. **8 -> 1**, behaviour-preserving (validate still runs first, so a codec-invalid duplicate still reports the codec error). No drift found, and reported as such: this is the *rule* exception the lens now carries — eight copies are eight chances to answer it differently later, and enforcing it on three paths of four would compile silently. Left measured and untouched: the caller contract's identical preamble minus the duplicate check (a semantics question, not a tidy-up) and the asymmetric zero-copy generics
- **[314](314-the-window-that-opened-too-late.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http — a red gate the owner pasted, taken ahead of the backlog and checked FIRST against rounds 308-313, which had touched this exact transport. **Not a regression**: the failing assertion reads `_pending.length`, a field the 308 router swap never went near. The bench was wrong — `_abortMidBody` destroys its socket immediately, so a request can be answered by a read ERROR promptly instead of by `bodyReadTimeout` at 2 s, and the test opened its observation window AFTER the four aborts. Under load all four are answered before the first poll and the guard reads 0 on a server that did everything right. The window now starts before the aborts. **Canary reproduced the reported failure exactly** — same message, same `Actual: <0>`, same ~12 s shape. Round 271's earlier de-flake had fixed the QUESTION (peak, not simultaneous count) and left the WINDOW; both print identical text, which is why it looked done
- **[313](313-two-of-three-were-testable.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart + rpc_dart_http2 — takes round 312's own "Not fixed" list of three unwitnessed fixes and finds **two of the three claims were wrong**. 309's "nothing automated reads log levels" — `LogController` exposes a public record stream, so four tests now pin the unified drain logging at its source, where all three servers inherit them. 311's "dead code has no runtime witness" — true of the deleted clause, false of the FUNCTION's contract, so four more pin `unconsumedWindowFor`, including the surprising clause that `null` does not disable the bound. Only 310 survives, and for a reason that is not the platform: `_WebMultiplexedChannel` is library-private and Dart privacy is per-library, so `src/`-importing cannot reach it the way it did in 307 → **B-31**. core 1429 -> 1433, http2 200 -> 204
- **[312](312-the-witness-308-shipped-without.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http2 — writes the regression test round 308 admitted it shipped without ("the test suite is not the witness here"). Drives the caller transport directly, since the endpoint asks for a stream once and cannot reach the second-call path — which is why 199 tests passed over the defect. Canary with the pre-308 form restored: **RpcStatusException(8) "Response exceeds the un-consumed window (4298316 > 4194304)" after consuming 4102938 bytes the reader had actually read**. Two traps found in the writing: the first version yielded `'z' * 256KiB` and passed on broken code because a one-character run deflates ~900:1 and never reached the bound, and `grpc-status 0` with 6060 bytes is not a passing call. Suite 199 -> 200
- **[311](311-a-fallback-that-could-never-fire.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart_http2 — asked whether more refactoring remains; ran RPC-25's step 1 across the transports and reports **two candidates DECLINED** (`RpcMessageParser`, `_notify` — identical, no drift) against one taken. `_fcWindow` was byte-identical in both http2 transports too, so the drift test cleared it — and reading it against the POLICY rather than its twin found a third `??` clause that **can never evaluate**, because `RpcSecurityPolicy`'s const default for the field is non-null. Two copies of dead code each restating the default as a literal, invisible to `dead_null_aware_expression` because the field is `int?` though the const value never is. Lens gains: **identical copies can both be wrong**, and most step-1 results are not findings

Run properly, the grep returns four identically-named fields and a byte-identical `close()`: the two channel variants are one lifecycle written twice. Two drifts, and the first is a live defect — the web copy answers a send failure with `await close()`, killing every in-flight call over one non-cloneable payload, which is exactly what the VM copy's own comment documents as wrong. Also finishes websocket's boundary: `grpcStatusFromWebSocketCloseCode` hidden, matching what 307 did to its http2 twin (surface 5 -> 4, 10 errors all in tests). **Different mechanism is not different abstraction**
- **[309](309-three-drains-one-of-them-quiet.md)** FIXED, rpc_dart + rpc_dart_websocket + rpc_dart_http + rpc_dart_http2 — RPC-25's second application, and the one that reaches **websocket**, which until now had been touched for documentation only. The drift was in the LOGGING rather than the logic: start-log `debug` on http against `info` on the other two, and only http2 ever reported the drain CONVERGED — so on two servers success was signalled by an absence. `_notify` was the other named candidate and is deliberately NOT merged: no drift, eight lines, and merging would add a core promise to save them. Derives **RPC-25** and applies it: four transports each hand-rolled the same per-stream router, **121 lines -> a 57-line `RpcStreamRouter` in core**, net **-64** in the transports. **The extraction is not the finding — the drift it exposed is**: the http2 caller returned an EXISTING stream unmetered where its own responder metered both paths, so a second consumer never discharged its flow-control budget and the call would be refused at the window for bytes it consumed. Two files that never import each other; the analyzer and the suite both green. The mandate's three clauses now each have a lens

- **[283](283-the-reset-is-the-cheap-arm.md)** CLEAN, rpc_dart_http2 — C-32's uncovered CPU half, refuted by the control moving FURTHER than the subject: the same 2000 streams not reset stall an unrelated connection 679 ms against the reset arm's 373 ms. - **[282](282-metadata-is-never-paced.md)** DEFERRED, rpc_dart — metadata is never paced AND nothing catches it: 4000 sends, 32 MiB, none blocked, against a control that parks at exactly the 64 KiB window. - **[281](281-the-asymmetry-that-is-not-there.md)** INCONCLUSIVE, rpc_dart — flow control is NOT the third instance of 279/280: `sendMetadata` spends no window and `_fcOnConsumed` returns on `bytes == 0`, so the two ends agree and nothing leaks.
